#### 11.4.3.11 Cyclic Tate periodicity and the local degree-two order

**Motivation.** The local norm index counts classes of fixed field units modulo norms. The local class formation uses ordinary second cohomology of the multiplicative group. The passage from one to the other changes the resolution and crosses the norm at the join between positive and negative degrees. These operations work for every module over a finite cyclic group. The local field hypotheses enter when counting the resulting classes.

**Examples to explain.** For the trivial action of $C_2=\langle s\rangle$ on $\mathbb Z$, the difference $s-1$ is zero and the norm is multiplication by $2$. Their alternating complex has $\mathbb Z/2$ in every even degree and zero in every odd degree. For the sign action, the difference is multiplication by $-2$ and the norm is zero, giving zero in every even degree and $\mathbb Z/2$ in every odd degree. We will relate these calculations to the original group cochains and exhibit the surviving classes.

**Definitions.** Let $k$ be a commutative ring, $G$ a finite group, and $M$ any left $k[G]$-module. Write $M^G$ for the fixed submodule, $I_GM$ for the span of the elements $gm-m$, and $N=\sum_{g\in G}g$. The bar resolution $B_j$ has free $k[G]$-basis $[g_1|\cdots|g_j]$ indexed by all tuples in $G^j$, including the empty tuple in degree zero. Its boundary is the alternating sum of the first face $g_1[g_2|\cdots|g_j]$, the interior faces multiplying adjacent entries, and the last face deleting $g_j$. Its augmentation sends $[]$ to $1$. Put $C^j=\operatorname{Hom}_{k[G]}(B_j,M)$ and $C_j=(M\otimes_k B_j)_G$, using diagonal action and coinvariants. The general Tate complex puts $C^j$ in degree $j\ge0$ and $C_j$ in degree $-j-1$, with the ordinary differentials on the two sides. Its map $C_0\to C^0$ is $N$ under the identifications by evaluation at $[]$ and by $[m\otimes []]\mapsto m$. The adjacent composites vanish because $N$ takes values in the invariants and kills every difference.

The differential out of $C^0$ measures $gm-m$ for every $g$. The boundary into $C_0$ has image $I_GM$ since the chain represented by $[m\otimes[g]]$ has boundary $g^{-1}m-m$. Hence the Tate groups in degrees $0,-1$ are $M^G/NM$ and $\ker N/I_GM$. Ordinary degree zero is $M^G$, and ordinary homological degree zero is $M/I_GM$. The same three terms compute Tate and ordinary cohomology in degree $q\ge1$, and Tate degree $-j-1$ and ordinary homology in degree $j\ge1$.

**Lemma (cyclic resolution).** Suppose $G=\langle\sigma\rangle$ has order $n\ge1$. Set $D=\sigma-1$ and $S_b(\sigma)=\sum_{i=0}^{b-1}\sigma^i$ for $b\ge0$, with $S_0=0$. Put $A=k[G]$. The alternating maps $d_j=D$ for odd $j$ and $d_j=N$ for positive even $j$, with the augmentation $\epsilon:A\to k$ in degree zero, define a free resolution $P_\sigma\to k$. [Lean, read]

*Proof.* Multiplication by $\sigma$ cycles the coefficients of $x=\sum_i a_i\sigma^i$. Therefore $\ker D=kN=\operatorname{im}N$ because $Nx=\epsilon(x)N$ and $N\cdot1=N$. The coefficient of $1$ in $N$ is $1$, so $\ker N=\ker\epsilon$. If $\epsilon(x)=0$, then $x=\sum_i a_i(\sigma^i-1)=D\sum_i a_i S_i(\sigma)$. Conversely $\epsilon D=0$, and $\epsilon$ is onto. These identities prove exactness over $k$. For any $M$ the same identity $\sigma^i-1=DS_i(\sigma)$ gives $I_GM=DM$, and $M^G=\ker D$. $\square$

**Theorem (comparison and period).** For every such $k,G,\sigma,M$ and every $q\in\mathbb Z$, the general Tate model has a comparison isomorphism $c_\sigma^q$ to the cohomology of the complex with $M$ in every degree and outgoing differential $D$ in even degrees and $N$ in odd degrees. The resulting quotients are $M^G/NM$ in even degree and $\ker N/DM$ in odd degree. The period isomorphism in the direction used by the archive is $p_\sigma^q=(c_\sigma^q)^{-1}c_\sigma^{q+2}$ from degree $q+2$ to degree $q$. Each of these maps commutes with every $k[G]$-linear coefficient map.

*Proof.* In homogeneous coordinates the bar basis and its translates are tuples $(h_0,\ldots,h_j)$ with diagonal action, corresponding to $h_0[h_0^{-1}h_1|\cdots|h_{j-1}^{-1}h_j]$. Its faces delete tuple entries. Inserting $1$ in front is a $k$-linear contraction of the augmented complex, with $1\in k$ sent to $(1)$ in the augmentation degree. Deleting the inserted entry gives the identity, and the other faces cancel in pairs against insertion after the boundary. Thus the free bar complex is exact.

To compare two of these resolutions, start with the map of the chosen degree-zero basis elements lifting the identity of $k$. For each basis element $e$ in source degree $j>0$, lift $F_{j-1}d_j(e)$ through the target boundary to define $F_j(e)$. The element is in the kernel by the chain identity, using the augmentation in degree one. Exactness supplies the lift and the free basis extends it $A$-linearly. Two maps $U,V$ lifting the identity are homotopic. Start with $h_{-1}=0$ and for each basis element lift $(U_j-V_j-h_{j-1}d_j)(e)$ through the target boundary to define $h_j(e)$. This element is a cycle by the homotopy equation in degree $j-1$, or equality on augmentation in degree zero. This recursion proves $U-V=dh+hd$. Applying it to the composites and identities proves the comparisons are homotopy inverses.

On cochains use precomposition with $P_\sigma\to B$. Under evaluation at $1$, precomposition by $D$ and by $N$ acts on $M$ by $D$ and $N$. On chains use $B\to P_{\sigma^{-1}}$ inside diagonal coinvariants. The isomorphism $(M\otimes_k A)_G\to M$ sends $[m\otimes g]$ to $g^{-1}m$ and has inverse $m\mapsto[m\otimes1]$. Thus the chain differential $\sigma^{-1}-1$ induces $D$ and the norm induces $N$ by permutation of the summation index. Both additive functors preserve the homotopy equations. At the splice both comparisons act by the identity on $M$, so they commute with the norm. They give a map of the spliced complexes inducing an isomorphism away from degrees $0,-1$ by the two homotopy equivalences. In degrees $0,-1$ they are the identity on the quotients, using $I_GM=DM$.

For $q=-j-1$ and $j\ge1$ the outgoing map is $D$ when $j$ is odd and $N$ when $j$ is even. This yields the claimed parity on both sides of the norm splice. Translation by two acts by the identity in the alternating complex. The comparisons use resolution maps independent of $M$ and commute with coefficient maps by precomposition and tensoring, including at the norm. For $n=1$ the maps are $D=0$ and $N=1$, giving zero in every Tate degree and in every strictly positive ordinary cohomological or homological degree. $\square$

**A degree-two comparison on cochains.** In degrees $0,1,2$ choose $P_\sigma\to B$ by sending the free generators to $[]$, $[\sigma]$, and $\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}[\sigma^i|\sigma]$. The first boundary is $D[]$. In the second boundary, $\sum_i(-[\sigma^{i+1}]+[\sigma^i])=0$, leaving $N[\sigma]$. Thus $f\in Z^2(G,M)$ maps to the class of $\sum_i f(\sigma^i,\sigma)$ in $M^G/NM$. A representative in the reverse direction for $m\in M^G$ is the carry cocycle
$$f_m(\sigma^i,\sigma^j)=\left\lfloor\frac{i+j}{n}\right\rfloor m\qquad (0\le i,j<n).$$
Each association of a triple addition produces total carry $\lfloor(i+j+t)/n\rfloor$, and the action fixes $m$. This proves the cocycle identity. When $n>1$ only $i=n-1$ contributes to the comparison sum and it contributes $m$. When $n=1$ the target quotient vanishes.

For the trivial $C_2$ module $\mathbb Z$, this cocycle equals $1$ on $(s,s)$ and zero on the other pairs. Its comparison is $1\bmod2$, proving it is a nonzero ordinary second class. On the sign module a $1$-cocycle is given by $f(1)=0$ and $f(s)=1$. The relation $1+s\cdot1=0$ verifies the cocycle identity at $(s,s)$, and a coboundary takes an even value at $s$. Its coordinate $1\bmod2$ also represents the nonzero class in degree $-1$, where $\ker N=\mathbb Z$ and $DM=2\mathbb Z$. In the trivial case the even quotient is $\mathbb Z/2$ and $\ker N=0$. In the sign case the even quotient is zero since $\ker D=0$.

**A finite module with nontrivial action.** Let $C_3=\langle\sigma\rangle$ act on $M=(\mathbb Z/3)^2$ by $\sigma(x,y)=(x+y,y)$. Then $D(x,y)=(y,0)$, $D^2=0$, $\sigma^3=1$, and $N=1+(1+D)+(1+2D)=0$. Both the fixed line and the difference image are spanned by $e_1=(1,0)$. Every even Tate group is a copy of $\mathbb Z/3$ generated by $e_1$. Every odd group is a copy of $\mathbb Z/3$ with coordinate the second component and generator the class of $e_2=(0,1)$. The carry cocycle with value $e_1$ on a carry gives a nonzero $H^2$ class since its comparison sum is $e_1$. A $1$-cocycle $f(\sigma^i)=S_i(\sigma)e_2$ takes the three values $0,e_2,e_1+2e_2$. The partial sums concatenate, and $Ne_2=0$ permits wraparound. Its generator value lies outside $DM$, giving the nonzero odd class.

**Changing the generator.** For $n>1$ let $\tau=\sigma^a$ with $1\le a<n$ and $\gcd(a,n)=1$. The augmented chain map $P_\tau\to P_\sigma$ multiplies by $a^j$ in degree $2j$ and $a^jS_a(\sigma)$ in degree $2j+1$ for $j\ge0$. The chain equations follow from $DS_a=\tau-1$ and $NS_a=aN$. The map lifts the identity, so it is the comparison up to homotopy. On positive cohomology it changes the even coordinate by $a^j$ and the odd coordinate by $a^{j+1}$, since $S_a$ acts as $a$ modulo $DM$. For the negative side use the same map $P_{\tau^{-1}}\to P_{\sigma^{-1}}$. In diagonal coinvariants it gives $a^j$ in chain degree $2j$ and induces $a^{j+1}$ on the fixed quotient in chain degree $2j+1$. This map goes from new chain coordinates to old, so the change from old to new uses the inverse. Both quotients are killed by $n$, since on invariants $N$ acts as $n$, and $N-n$ is divisible by $D$. Thus $a$ acts invertibly on each quotient. Including the canonical comparisons at $0,-1$, one obtains $c_\tau^q=a^{\lceil q/2\rceil}c_\sigma^q$ for every integer $q$, and $p_\tau^q=a p_\sigma^q$. In the finite-module example, replacing $\sigma$ by $\sigma^2$ doubles the degree-two coordinate, as $\sum_i f_{e_1}(\sigma^{2i},\sigma^2)=2e_1$. For $n=1$ there is only one generator. A group isomorphism and a compatible coefficient isomorphism transport the comparisons to those for the transported generator, by transport of the resolutions and homotopy uniqueness. Coefficient naturality itself holds for any coefficient homomorphism at fixed group and generator.

**Why cyclicity matters.** Take $G=C_2\times C_2$ with trivial coefficients $\mathbb Z$. Degree-zero Tate cohomology is $\mathbb Z/4$. Pull back the $C_2$ carry cocycle along each projection. Each class is killed by $2$ because twice the carry is the coboundary of the corresponding projection lifted to $\{0,1\}$. Restriction to the two coordinate subgroups distinguishes the two generators. They span a subgroup isomorphic to $C_2\times C_2$ of $H^2(G,\mathbb Z)$. Since $\mathbb Z/4$ has no such subgroup, degree two and Tate degree zero cannot be isomorphic. At the resolution step a nonidentity $s$ fixes independent constant-coefficient vectors on the two cosets of $\langle s\rangle$ in the regular module. The full norm image spans only their sum, so the proposed resolution fails exactness.

**The local consumer.** [Lean, read] Let $L/K$ be a finite cyclic Galois extension of nonarchimedean local fields with compatible valuations. Take $M=\operatorname{Additive}(L^\times)$ with its Galois action. The additive norm corresponds to the product of all conjugates. At lines 19–57 the quotient map sends a fixed additive class to the corresponding multiplicative norm class. Switching the order of the difference in the quotient relation replaces a norm witness by its inverse. The norm-index theorem of [§11.4.3.10](#114310-cyclic-hilbert-90-and-the-local-norm-index) (LocalFields/HerbrandLocal.lean:65–79) gives the order of that multiplicative quotient, so $|H^2(G,L^\times)|=[L:K]$. The positivity of the degree ensures that the archive’s natural cardinality represents the actual order. The isomorphism with the norm quotient uses the selected generator, while the order is independent of that choice.

#### 11.4.3.12 The Tate cup product by augmentation shifts

**Motivation.** The cyclic complex in [§11.4.3.11](#114311-cyclic-tate-periodicity-and-the-local-degree-two-order) shows where the norm sits and gives coordinates for the examples below. For a general finite group, the augmentation sequence lets us move a class into ordinary cohomology. Ordinary cup products multiply cochains. A negative Tate degree represents a chain, and the norm joins the chain and cochain complexes. To multiply across this join, change each low-degree input coefficient by an augmentation sequence until both inputs have positive degree. Bring the output back by the inverse connecting maps. The two choices of shift require a sign rule that holds even at the norm.

**Examples.** For $C_2$ with trivial $\mathbb Z/2$ coefficients, a degree $-1$ class times a degree $0$ class can give a nonzero degree $-1$ class. For $C_3$ with trivial $\mathbb Z/3$ coefficients, the right shift in bidegree $(1,0)$ reverses the sign of a nonzero class. Both examples use coefficient modules that are nonflat over $\mathbb Z$. The split augmentation sequences make these calculations possible.

**Definitions.** Let $k$ be any commutative ring and $G$ any finite group. Write $T^i(X)=\widehat H^i(G,X)$ for a left $k[G]$-module $X$. All coefficient tensor products are over $k$ with diagonal action. Use ordinary bar cochains in degrees $i\ge0$, ordinary bar chains of degree $j$ in degree $-j-1$, and the norm from $-1$ to $0$. In each degree the underlying module of the Tate complex is a direct sum of finitely many copies of $X$ in bar coordinates, so an equivariant short exact sequence induces a degreewise short exact sequence of Tate complexes. For $E=(0\to X'\to X\to X''\to0)$ let $\delta_E$ denote its connecting map. Lift a cocycle from $X''$, differentiate the lift, and take the preimage under the inclusion of $X'$.

Put $P=k[G]$, let $\epsilon:P\to k$ sum the coefficients, and set $I=\ker\epsilon$ and $SX=I\otimes X$. The augmentation sequence $U_X$ has terms $SX,P\otimes X,X$, with maps induced by inclusion and augmentation. Put $s_X=\delta_{U_X}$, $L_{A,B}=\delta_{U_A\otimes B}$ and $R_{A,B}=\delta_{A\otimes U_B}$. These raise the degree by one and have target coefficients $SX$, $(SA)\otimes B$ and $A\otimes(SB)$, respectively. Let $e(p)=(-1)^p$ be the image in $k$ of the integral parity unit. For all integers $p$, $e(p+1)=-e(p)$.

**Lemma 1 (the augmentation shift).** The three sequences defining $s,L,R$ are short exact for every $X,A,B$, and their middle terms have zero Tate groups. Thus $s,L,R$ are isomorphisms. [Lean, read] GroundTheory/TateCohomology/AugmentationSES.lean:31–90,284–361 and CupTateShift.lean:51–79.

*Proof.* The section $1\mapsto 1_G$ of $\epsilon$ and the complementary retraction $v\mapsto v-\epsilon(v)1_G$ give a $k$-linear splitting which survives arbitrary tensoring. The maps of the exact sequences themselves are equivariant. The elements $g-1_G$ for $g\ne1_G$ form a $k$-basis of $I$. Each middle term is equivariantly isomorphic after reassociation and permutation to $P\otimes Y$. The map $g\otimes y\mapsto g\otimes g^{-1}y$ untwists the diagonal action into left translation on the first factor alone.

For acyclicity, in homogeneous bar coordinates an equivariant cochain into this permutation module is determined by its coefficient at $1_G$ on each tuple, with alternating deletion differential. On the chain side, diagonal coinvariants of $P\otimes Y\otimes B_j$, where $B_j$ is the homogeneous bar module, identify with $Y\otimes B_j$ by translating each tuple by the inverse of the regular basis index. The augmented tuple complex contracts by insertion of $1_G$ at the front. The first deletion gives the identity, and the other deletions cancel insertion after deletion. This contracts the augmented chains and, by applying $\operatorname{Hom}_k(-,Y)$ to the same contraction, the augmented cochains. The norm sends the coinvariants, identified with $Y$ by summing coordinates, isomorphically to the constant invariant vectors. This supplies vanishing at the splice as well. The Tate long exact sequence gives the three isomorphisms. The archive obtains acyclicity by summing conjugates of the projection onto the identity coordinate. [Lean, read] InducedNorm.lean:17–53,61–89 and DimensionShift.lean:16–22.

**Lemma 2 (two connecting maps).** In a commutative three-by-three array of cochain complexes whose rows and columns are all short exact, the two composites of connecting maps from the bottom right to the top left are negatives of one another.

*Proof.* Index rows and columns by $1,2,3$ and call the horizontal and vertical maps $h,v$. Lift a bottom-right cycle $z$ to $x_{32}$ and then to $x_{22}$. Set $x_{23}=h x_{22}$, another lift of $z$. Write $d x_{23}=vb$ with $b$ at $(1,3)$, lift $b$ to $c$ at $(1,2)$, and write $dc=hw$ with $w$ at $(1,1)$. This gives $[w]$ by the vertical-then-horizontal route. Now $d x_{22}-vc$ maps to zero horizontally, so it equals $he$ for $e$ at $(2,1)$. Applying $v$ gives $hve=d x_{32}$ since two successive vertical arrows compose to zero. Thus $ve$ represents the first horizontal connecting class. Finally $hde=-vdc=-vhw=-hvw$, so injectivity gives $de=-vw$. The other route gives $[-w]$. Each preimage used to represent a connecting class is a cycle by $d^2=0$ and injectivity of the corresponding inclusion. Applying this argument to the degreewise exact Tate complexes gives the sign in every degree. [Lean, read] TateConnecting.lean:82–91,133–191,193–283.

For a connecting identity with arbitrary $E$, require $E$ and the relevant tensor sequence $E\otimes B$ or $A\otimes E$ to be short exact. Flatness of the unshifted tensor factor over $k$ suffices for the second requirement. For the induction below, inserting $I$ or $P$ into a tensor placement of a sequence already known to be exact preserves exactness by freeness and by associativity and symmetry of tensor products. In the grid for $E$ and $U_B$, the rows $X_i\otimes U_B$ are split over $k$. The columns are $E$ tensored with $SB,P\otimes B,B$ and are exact by the given tensor exactness and the insertion argument. The grid for $U_A$ and $E$ uses the corresponding exact sequences with the factors reversed. For two connections in the same coefficient slot, use the rows $U_{X_i}$, optionally tensored with the other coefficient. The columns then come from $E$, $I\otimes E$ and $P\otimes E$, with the other coefficient included when needed. These are the five instances of Lemma 2 in CupTateShift.lean:178–246. [Lean, read] CupTateShift.lean:20–45,134–246 and AugmentationSES.lean:143–253.

**Theorem (extension by augmentation).** For every $k,G$ as above, all $A,B$ and integers $p,q$, there is a $k$-bilinear product $T^p(A)\times T^q(B)\to T^{p+q}(A\otimes B)$ agreeing with the ordinary cup product under the positive comparisons when $p,q>0$. Its two connecting identities are

- $\delta_{E\otimes B}(a\smile b)=(\delta_E a)\smile b$ for $a\in T^p(X'')$ and $b\in T^q(B)$.
- $\delta_{A\otimes E}(a\smile b)=e(p)\,a\smile(\delta_E b)$ for $a\in T^p(A)$ and $b\in T^q(X'')$.

Each formula assumes $E$ and its displayed tensor are short exact. Positive agreement and the two augmentation instances alone determine the family uniquely. Eligible left and right shifts may be taken in any order.

*Positive base.* The ordinary cochain formula evaluates $f$ on the first $p$ group entries and tensors it with $g$ on the last $q$ entries, acted on by the product of the first $p$ entries. Equivalently, in homogeneous coordinates evaluate on vertices $0,\ldots,p$ and $p,\ldots,p+q$. In the alternating deletion sum on one more vertex, deletions $0,\ldots,p$ belong to $df\smile g$, and deletions $p+1,\ldots,p+q+1$ belong to $e(p)f\smile dg$. The last deletion from the first summand and the first deletion from the second summand produce the same extra term with opposite signs. Thus $d(f\smile g)=df\smile g+e(p)f\smile dg$. This descends to cohomology because cups of cycles are cycles, and a cup of a boundary and a cycle is a boundary by the same formula. To compute a connecting map, lift the cochain in the slot of $E$ and cup it with the cocycle in the other slot. Equivariance makes this a lift of the product. Differentiation leaves just the term from the lifted slot, with coefficient $1$ on the left and $e(p)$ on the right. Tensor exactness gives the preimage computing the connecting class. In strictly positive degrees the Tate and ordinary maps compute the connection by the same lift and differential. [Lean, read] CupCochains.lean:102–204, CupProduct.lean:70–155, CupConnecting.lean:19–152, TateConnecting.lean:95–127 and CupTateShift.lean:88–94,262–272.

*Construction.* If $p\le0$, define the product by $L_{A,B}^{-1}(s_Aa\smile b)$ using the product at $(p+1,q)$. Otherwise if $q\le0$, use $R_{A,B}^{-1}(e(p)\,a\smile s_Bb)$ using $(p,q+1)$. Otherwise use the positive base. Every recursive step decreases $\max(1-p,0)+\max(1-q,0)$ by one. The stopping pair is $(\max(p,1),\max(q,1))$, and the changes in coefficient modules do not enter the measure. Each step preserves bilinearity. [Lean, read] CupTateShift.lean:61–132.

*Proof of the connecting identities.* Induct on that measure, allowing every short exact sequence and eligible coefficient at each smaller measure. The positive base was proved above. Apply the output shift at the target of the connecting identity and use Lemma 2 to commute it through the connecting map on the left side.

1. **Left recursion, left connecting map.** After applying $L$, the left side becomes $-\delta_{(I\otimes E)\otimes B}(s_{X''}a\smile b)$. Induction at $(p+1,q)$ turns it into $-(\delta_{I\otimes E}s_{X''}a)\smile b$. On the right side, $L$ comes from $U_{X'}\otimes B$. The left connecting identity at $(p+1,q)$ for $U_{X'}$ gives $(s_{X'}\delta_E a)\smile b$. Lemma 2 on the input identifies the values, including when the target product uses a different recursive branch.
2. **Right recursion, left connecting map.** Here $p>0$ and $q\le0$. After applying $R$, induction at $(p,q+1)$ with coefficient $SB$ gives $-e(p)(\delta_Ea)\smile s_B b$. The target product at $(p+1,q)$ right-shifts with factor $e(p+1)=-e(p)$.
3. **Left recursion, right connecting map.** Here $p\le0$. After applying $L$, induction at $(p+1,q)$ with coefficient $SA$ gives $-e(p+1)s_Aa\smile\delta_E b$. The target product at $(p,q+1)$ left-shifts and has the external factor $e(p)$ from the identity to be proved.
4. **Right recursion, right connecting map.** After applying $R$, the first side becomes $-e(p)\delta_{A\otimes(I\otimes E)}(a\smile s_{X''}b)$. Induction at $(p,q+1)$ gives $-e(p)^2a\smile\delta_{I\otimes E}s_{X''}b$. The right side uses the right connecting identity at $(p,q+1)$ for $U_{X'}$ and gives $e(p)^2a\smile s_{X'}\delta_Eb$. Lemma 2 on the input makes these equal.

In case 1, when $p=0$, the target first degree is $1$. It now uses the right shift if $q\leq 0$ and the positive cup if $q>0$. The second induction call, for $U_{X'}$, gives its image under $L$ in either branch. In case 4, when $q=0$, the target pair $(p,1)$ is positive. The separate induction call for $U_{X'}$ again gives its shifted image.

The exactness checks preceding the theorem justify each sequence in the induction, and Lemma 1 allows cancellation of each applied shift. Taking $E=U_A$ or $U_B$ gives both shift formulas at all degrees. Each eligible shift recovers the same value from a smaller measure, giving order independence. Any other family satisfying those same augmentation identities and positive agreement must obey the same recursion with the same base. [Lean, read] CupTateShift.lean:276–382. The archive proves the two inductions separately. CupTate.lean:102–143 exposes uniqueness.

**Hand calculation 1 (a negative input for $C_2$).** Take $k=\mathbb Z$, $G=\langle s\rangle=C_2$ and $A=B=F=\mathbb Z/2$ with trivial action. Identify $F\otimes F$ with $F$. Write $t=s-1$. The first shifted coefficient is one-dimensional over $F$ with basis $t$ and trivial action, since $st=-t$. The same holds for each iterated shift with basis $t^{\otimes j}$. For a trivial one-dimensional $F$-coefficient every Tate group is $F$ in the cyclic coordinates. Lift a basis vector $v$ along augmentation to $1_G\otimes v$. Both $D=s-1$ and $N=1+s$ on this lift equal $t\otimes v$ modulo $2$. Thus each shift takes the basis class to the shifted basis class.

Start at $(-1,0)$ with two classes $1$. Left-first goes through $(0,0),(1,0),(1,1)$. Right-first goes through $(-1,1),(0,1),(1,1)$. Both arrive at normalized positive $1$-cocycles sending $s$ to their shifted basis vectors. Their cup takes the product basis at $(s,s)$ and is zero at all other pairs. Its degree-two coordinate, the sum of its values at $(1_G,s)$ and $(s,s)$, is $1$. Every sign acts as $1$, and all inverse shifts remove the added basis factors. The product is $1$ in $T^{-1}(F)$ by either route.

**Hand calculation 2 (a visible sign for $C_3$).** Take $k=\mathbb Z$, $G=\langle s\rangle=C_3$ and $A=B=F=\mathbb Z/3$ with trivial action. Write $e_i=s^i$ for the basis modulo $3$, $t_i=e_{i+1}-e_i$ with indices modulo $3$, $J=I\otimes F$ and $u=e_0+e_1+e_2\in J$. On trivial $F$ both $D,N$ vanish. On $J$ the norm vanishes and the fixed line is $F u$. The class $1$ in degree zero shifts to the cocycle $h(s^i)=e_i-e_0$, obtained by lifting to $e_0$ and applying the coboundary.

First take $f(s^i)=i$ in $T^1(F)$ and $1\in T^0(F)$. The right output shift sends $1\in T^1(F)$ to $u\in T^2(J)$ by taking the norm of the lift $e_0$. The positive cup $f\smile h$ has degree-two coordinate $\sum_{i=0}^2 i t_i=t_1+2t_2=-u$. The factor $e(1)=-1$ changes this to $u$, so the product in degree one is $1$. Omitting the sign gives $-1$.

Now start with $1,1$ in bidegree $(0,0)$. Put $W=\sum_i e_i\otimes t_i$ and $V=\sum_i t_i\otimes e_i$. They belong to $J\otimes_F J$ since all row and column sums are zero. They are invariant under simultaneous cyclic translation. The left-then-right output shifts apply $D$ to the first lift to give $t_0$, then $N$ to $t_0\otimes e_0$ to give $V$. The reverse order gives $W$. The positive cup $h\smile h$ has coordinate $\sum_i(e_i-e_0)\otimes t_i=W$. In degree two, $W+V=u\otimes u$ since the diagonal entries are $-2$ and the off-diagonal entries are $1$ in $F$. Put $Q_d=\sum_i e_i\otimes e_{i+d}$ with indices modulo $3$. Then $N(t_0\otimes t_0)=2Q_0-Q_1-Q_2$ and $N(t_0\otimes t_1)=2Q_1-Q_0-Q_2$. Both have all entries $2$ in $F$. Translation and transposition give the remaining two norms for the basis $t_0,t_1$. Consequently the norm image is $F(u\otimes u)$, and $[V]=-[W]\ne0$ since $W$ has nonconstant entries. Left-then-right uses $e(1)=-1$ so its signed target is $-[W]=[V]$. Right-then-left uses $e(0)=1$ so its target is $[W]$. Both inverses give $1$ in $T^0(F)$. These calculations use the cyclic comparison with $D=s-1$ and degree-two evaluation $\sum_i c(s^i,s)$.

**Failure boundary.** Give all coefficients trivial $C_2$ action and take $k=\mathbb Z$. The short exact sequence $0\to\mathbb Z\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb Z\to\mathbb Z/2\to0$ becomes a zero first arrow on a nonzero module followed by an isomorphism after tensoring on either side with $\mathbb Z/2$. The output sequence therefore has no connecting map of the prescribed short-exact-sequence kind. The cup product still exists, and the same nonflat coefficient preserves every linearly split augmentation sequence.

#### 11.4.3.13 The degree-zero unit of the Tate cup product

**Motivation.** In ordinary cohomology the identity coefficient is the invariant scalar $1$. In Tate cohomology the norm enters the complex immediately before degree zero. The identity is therefore the *class* of that scalar. The job of the two dimension shifts is to transport the identity law from degree zero across both halves of the complex, including the norm differential.

**Examples.** For $C_2$ over the integers, the identity has two representatives $1$ and $3$ and fixes a nonzero class in degree $-2$. For $C_3$ over $\mathbb F_3$, the norm on the trivial representation vanishes and the identity fixes a nonzero class already in degree $-1$.

**Definition.** Let $k$ be any commutative ring with identity, let $G$ be a finite group, and let $X$ be any left $k[G]$-module. Write $T^i(X)=\widehat H^i(G,X)$ and let $k$ itself carry the trivial action. All coefficient tensors are over $k$ with diagonal action. The degree-zero cochain is identified with its value in $X$, and its differential takes $x$ to the function $g\mapsto gx-x$. The preceding differential, from degree $-1$, takes $a$ to $N_X a=\sum_{g\in G} ga$. Reindexing the sum shows that $N_X a$ is fixed. Thus degree-zero cycles are $X^G$ and degree-zero coboundaries are exactly $N_X X$. In particular,
$$
T^0(X)=X^G/N_X X,\qquad
T^0(k)=k/|G|k,\qquad
1_T=[1]\in T^0(k).
$$
The identification is natural because equivariant linear maps preserve fixed elements and commute with the norm. In the archive the natural identification is `tateCohomologyZeroIso`, and `tateOne` applies its inverse to the quotient class of the invariant $1$. [Lean, read] `TateLow.lean:131–237` and `TateCupUnit.lean:58–60`.

**Lemma 1 (degree zero and representatives).** If $x\in A^G$ and $y\in B^G$ then $[x]\smile[y]=[x\otimes y]$ in $T^0(A\otimes B)$. This expression is independent of both choices.

*Proof.* The pure tensor is invariant. Changing the first representative by $N_A a$ adds $N_{A\otimes B}(a\otimes y)$ since $y$ is fixed. Changing the second representative by $N_B b$ adds $N_{A\otimes B}(x\otimes b)$ since $x$ is fixed. Both steps can be applied in succession because each changed representative is still invariant.

To identify this quotient formula with the constructed cup, use the two augmentation shifts of [§11.4.3.12](#114312-the-tate-cup-product-by-augmentation-shifts) as injective tests on the output. First shift the $A$ slot, then the $B$ slot. In ordinary degree zero the cochains on the unique empty tuple multiply to $x\otimes y$. The first connecting map sends its class to the ordinary product of the first shifted class with $y$, by the left connecting identity. The second connecting map yields the negative of the product of the two shifted degree-one classes, by the right identity with first degree $1$. At the crossing from degree zero to degree one the ordinary and Tate connections use the same lifted zero-cochain and the same differential. Once in positive degree they agree as well. This is precisely the signed image of the Tate product by the two augmentation equations. The two isomorphisms can be cancelled. The archive implements these steps in `tateCup_zero_zero`, using the inverse degree-zero quotient comparisons on the three cycles and the positive comparison in bidegree $(1,1)$. [Lean, read] `CupTate.lean:13–98`, `TateConnecting.lean:82–127` and `CupTateShift.lean:386–419`.

**Lemma 2 (transport of connecting maps by unitors).** Write $\lambda_X(c\otimes x)=cx$ and $\rho_X(x\otimes c)=cx$. For each short exact sequence $E=(0\to X'\to X\to X''\to0)$, both $k\otimes E$ and $E\otimes k$ are short exact. In every integer degree $i$ their connecting maps satisfy
$$
\delta_E T^i(\lambda_{X''})=T^{i+1}(\lambda_{X'})\delta_{k\otimes E},
\qquad
\delta_E T^i(\rho_{X''})=T^{i+1}(\rho_{X'})\delta_{E\otimes k}.
$$

*Proof.* The inverse maps tensor with $1$ in the indicated position. The formulas are equivariant because $k$ has trivial action, and commute with both arrows because the arrows are linear. They give isomorphisms of the short complexes, which transfer exactness. Under such a diagram, applying the middle unitor to a lift of a cycle gives a lift of its transported cycle. The differential commutes with the unitor, and the preimage in the first term is transported by the first unitor. This gives the two displayed identities even when the differential is the norm. In Lean the two `ShortComplex.isoMk` constructions transport exactness by `ShortComplex.shortExact_of_iso`; the equations apply connecting naturality to the isomorphisms of short complexes. Both the original and the tensor exactness witnesses occur in the connecting statements. [Lean, read] `TateCupUnit.lean:18–50`.

**Lemma 3 (two classes of shift).** Put $P=k[G]$ and $I=\ker(P\xrightarrow{\epsilon}k)$, with $\epsilon$ the augmentation. For any $X$, the augmentation sequence $U_X=(0\to I\otimes X\to P\otimes X\to X\to0)$ has an isomorphism $s_X^i:T^i(X)\to T^{i+1}(I\otimes X)$ as its connecting map. There is also a short exact sequence $V_X=(0\to X\to C_X\to Q_X\to0)$ with connecting isomorphism $t_X^i:T^i(Q_X)\to T^{i+1}(X)$.

*Proof.* Use the augmentation shift already proved in [§11.4.3.12](#114312-the-tate-cup-product-by-augmentation-shifts) for $s_X^i$. For the other shift let $C_X=\operatorname{Map}(G,X)$ with action $(g f)(h)=f(hg)$ on functions into the underlying $k$-module. The map $j(x)(h)=hx$ is equivariant since $j(gx)(h)=hgx$ and injective by evaluation at the identity. Define $Q_X=C_X/j(X)$, giving exactness. Since $G$ is finite, $C_X$ is identified with $P$ tensored with the underlying $k$-module of $X$ with trivial action. Explicitly, $g\otimes x$ corresponds to the function supported at $g^{-1}$ with value $x$. The inherited vanishing for regular permutation coefficients gives $T^i(C_X)=0$ in every degree. Exactness of the long sequence on either side of the connector proves that $t_X^i$ is an isomorphism. This is the concrete coinduction from the trivial subgroup used by the archive, which supplies the vanishing via `tateAcyclic_coind_bot`. [Lean, read] `DimensionShift.lean:16–22,30–44,46–54,79–98` and `Induced.lean:94–139`.

**Theorem (identity in every degree).** For every commutative $k$, finite $G$, left $k[G]$-module $X$, integer $q$ and $b\in T^q(X)$,
$$
T^q(\lambda_X)(1_T\smile b)=b,
\qquad
T^q(\rho_X)(b\smile1_T)=b.
$$
The left equality is `leftUnitor_tateCup_tateOne`. The right equality follows by the parallel argument below.

*Proof.* Let $L_X^q$ and $R_X^q$ be the two left sides, viewed as linear endomorphisms in the variable $b$. Lemma 1 followed by the respective unitor shows that both send $[y]$ to $[y]$ at zero. This proves the base for every $X$ by surjectivity of the quotient map.

For any $E$ as in Lemma 2 and every integer $i$, the right-slot connecting identity from [§11.4.3.12](#114312-the-tate-cup-product-by-augmentation-shifts) with first degree $0$ gives
$$
\delta_E L^i_{X''}=L^{i+1}_{X'}\delta_E .
$$
Here the output connection uses $k\otimes E$, separately known short exact by Lemma 2, and the sign is $(-1)^0=1$. To get $\delta_E R^i_{X''}=R^{i+1}_{X'}\delta_E$, use $E\otimes k$ and the left-slot identity, which has coefficient $1$ in every first degree. The maps on the outputs use the first-term unitors of Lemma 2. This argument keeps the degree of $1_T$ fixed throughout.

Suppose the identity law holds at $i+1$ for every coefficient. Apply the identities just obtained to $E=U_X$. The composites of $L_X^i$ and $R_X^i$ with $s_X^i$ are both $s_X^i$, using the law for $I\otimes X$ in degree $i+1$. Injectivity proves the law at $i$. Starting at zero and repeating gives every negative integer, with exactly $-q$ augmentation steps to check degree $q<0$.

Suppose instead the identity law holds at $i$ for every coefficient. Apply it to $Q_X$ in $E=V_X$. For $b\in T^{i+1}(X)$ choose $c=(t_X^i)^{-1}b$. The identities give $L_X^{i+1}(b)=t_X^i L_{Q_X}^i(c)=t_X^i(c)=b$ and $R_X^{i+1}(b)=t_X^i R_{Q_X}^i(c)=b$. Starting at zero gives every positive integer, in $q$ steps for degree $q>0$. The archive's successor proof makes this choice, and its predecessor proof cancels the augmentation map. Both generalize the coefficient object before integer induction. [Lean, read] `TateCupUnit.lean:64–129`.

The representative of $1_T$ can be any $1+|G|r$, with $r\in k$. The two terms differ by $N_k r$, so these representatives define the same input in every degree. For a general class $b$, replacing a cycle by that cycle plus a coboundary also preserves the input. More explicitly, under the connecting construction a coboundary $d a$ lifts to $d\widetilde a$ since the sequence is surjective on cochains in the preceding degree. Its differential is zero. Two lifts of a fixed cycle differ by a cochain in the first term, whose differential changes the resulting representative by a coboundary there. This explains representative independence through the two shifts even at degree $-1$. The cup and the unitors used above already act on the resulting quotient classes.

**Hand calculation 1 (trivial integral coefficients for $C_2$).** Let $G=\langle s\rangle=C_2$ and $k=X=\mathbb Z$. Use the cyclic comparison of [§11.4.3.11](#114311-cyclic-tate-periodicity-and-the-local-degree-two-order) in which the differential out of even degree is $D=s-1$ and out of odd degree is $N=1+s$. Here $D=0$ and $N=2$. The zero quotient is $\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z$ and both $1$ and $3$ represent $1_T$. Degree $-1$ is zero since $\ker(2)=0$. Degree $-2$ is $\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z$ with nonzero class $[1]$.

In the first augmentation sequence use $e=1_G\in P$ as lift of $1$. Its degree $-2$ differential is $D e=t=s-1$, so the shifted class is $[t]$ in degree $-1$ of $I=\mathbb Z t$. On $I$ the action is $st=-t$, hence $N=0$ and $D=-2$. The class is nonzero modulo $2t$. The next lift is $e\otimes t$, and its degree $-1$ differential is
$$
N(e\otimes t)=e\otimes t+s\otimes(-t)=-t\otimes t.
$$
In $I\otimes I$ the action is trivial and the norm is again $2$, so the twice-shifted class is $[-t\otimes t]$ in a zero-degree group of order two. Left multiplication by the unit gives that same class because $1\otimes(-t\otimes t)$ is sent to $-t\otimes t$. The right unitor does the same to $(-t\otimes t)\otimes1$. Applying the inverses of the two connecting maps gives $1_T\smile[1]=[1]=[1]\smile1_T$ after the unitors in degree $-2$. Replacing either occurrence of the scalar $1$ at the start by $3$ changes the corresponding final coefficient by an even multiple of the generator.

**Hand calculation 2 (trivial coefficients over $\mathbb F_3$ for $C_3$).** Let $G=\langle s\rangle=C_3$ and $k=X=\mathbb F_3$. Here both $D$ and $N$ vanish. Every cyclic Tate group is $\mathbb F_3$, and the degree-zero quotient is $\mathbb F_3/0$ with unit $[1]$. Write $e_j=s^j$ for $j=0,1,2$ in $P$, with indices modulo $3$. The vector $u=e_0+e_1+e_2$ belongs to $I$ since its augmentation is zero. For $v=\sum_j a_j e_j$ one has $Nv=(\sum_j a_j)u$. Thus $N I=0$. Translation fixes $v$ exactly when all $a_j$ are equal, so $I^G=\mathbb F_3 u$ and the zero Tate quotient there is the same line.

Take the degree $-1$ class $[2]$. The augmentation lift $2e_0$ has differential $N(2e_0)=2u$. Multiplying $[2u]$ by $[1]$ in degree zero and applying either unitor returns $[2u]$. The norm computation gives the inverse shift explicitly as $[a u]\mapsto[a]$. It follows that both unit products in degree $-1$ are $[2]$. This computation is over the coefficient ring $\mathbb F_3$. In either example the degree-zero quotient has a nonzero unit, and the indicated negative-degree class is nonzero in its original group.

**Remarks.** The difference between the ordinary and Tate unit has an exact boundary. The ordinary invariant scalar $1$ maps to zero in Tate degree zero exactly when $|G|1_k$ is a unit of $k$. Indeed $[1]=0$ is equivalent to $1\in |G|k$. If $|G|$ is invertible and $x\in X^G$, then $x=N_X(|G|^{-1}x)$, so $T^0(X)=0$, and both shifts propagate this vanishing to all degrees. For $C_2$ over $\mathbb Q$ the ordinary invariant algebra is $\mathbb Q$ and its unit is nonzero, while $1=N_k(1/2)$ represents zero in Tate degree zero. In particular there is no unital comparison homomorphism in the reverse direction to ordinary invariants in this example. Even when $1_T\ne0$, omitting norm coboundaries gives the wrong class relation. For $C_2$ over $\mathbb Z$, multiplying $1$ and $3$ separately by $1$ in the ordinary invariants gives different integers although the two products represent the same Tate class.

The integral case has a classical source in Milne, *Class Field Theory*, II §3, pp. 77–80. The group is finite in that section, the norm is the group sum, the zero group is the invariant quotient, and Proposition 3.1 and the paragraph following it give the two directions of dimension shifting. Proposition 1.38 in II §1, p. 73, supplies the ordinary degree-zero pairing and the connecting sign with the two tensor exactness conditions over $\mathbb Z$. The paragraph “Cup-products” on p. 80 records the all-degree extension and refers its proof to Weiss. The arbitrary commutative ring and the explicit unitor transports above follow the archive argument. [Milne, *Class Field Theory*, v4.03](https://www.jmilne.org/math/CourseNotes/CFT.pdf)

**Where the identity is used.** In `TateCup.lean:69–105` the two input coefficients at the start are both the trivial integral representation of a finite subgroup. Its left and right unitors coincide on $k\otimes k$ by commutativity of scalar multiplication. The proof rewrites the left identity as a right-unitor expression with the *unit still in the first cup slot*. It then applies two left-slot connecting identities, each time using right-unitor exactness and naturality. As the unit moves from degree $0$ to degree $2$, the other class stays in degree $n$. The normalization theorem in [§11.4.3.14](#114314-the-zero-degree-normalization-of-tates-isomorphism) identifies the resulting first class with the restriction of the chosen degree-two class. The splitting-module application in [§11.4.3.15](#114315-detecting-cohomological-triviality-on-subgroups) supplies the acyclicity needed for the two maps to be isomorphisms. The resulting cup formula feeds `ClassFormations/HighFinite.lean:68–92` at $(2,n)$ with $n>0$, where the positive comparison returns ordinary cohomology. This part uses the class-formation isomorphism under its subgroup hypotheses and the group-order annihilation theorem. [Source ledger and consumer table](../sources/SOURCES.md#chapter-11-114313-the-degree-zero-unit-of-the-tate-cup-product)

#### 11.4.3.14 The zero-degree normalization of Tate's isomorphism

**Motivation.** The long exact sequences of two extensions can identify cohomology groups two degrees apart. To use the identification in a cup product, we must know which degree-two class is the image of the degree-zero unit. An isomorphism of abstract cyclic groups leaves that choice undetermined. Here the two extensions specify it through their connecting maps.

**Examples.** For $C_2$ with trivial integer coefficients the two connecting maps take the class of $1$ to the order-two carry cocycle. For $C_3$ with trivial coefficients $\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z$, the same carry has order three and detects a reversal of either connecting sign.

**Definitions.** Let $G$ be finite, $M$ a left $\mathbb Z[G]$-module, and $u\in H^2(G,M)$. Write $T_S^j(X)=\widehat H^j(S,X)$ for $S\le G$, with restricted coefficients understood, and put $u_S=\operatorname{res}^G_S u$. Let $P=\mathbb Z[G]$, with basis $e_g$ and left translation, and $I=\ker(\epsilon:P\to\mathbb Z)$, where $\epsilon(e_g)=1$. The connecting convention in this section is lift, differentiate, then take the preimage under the injection. In degrees zero and one the ordinary differentials are
$$
(dy)(s)=sy-y,\qquad (db)(s,t)=s b(t)-b(st)+b(s).
$$
The unit $\mathbf 1_S\in T_S^0(\mathbb Z)$ is the class of $1$ in $\mathbb Z/|S|\mathbb Z$. The coefficient-ring unit from [§11.4.3.13](#114313-the-degree-zero-unit-of-the-tate-cup-product) at $\mathbb Z$ and the archive's specialized `tateOneClass` are definitionally equal. Both apply the same inverse quotient comparison to the invariant scalar $1$. `TateCup.lean:65` uses this equality directly to apply `tateTheoremIso_zero_apply`.

**Lemma 1 (the restricted augmentation).** The sequence $E=(0\to I\to P\to\mathbb Z\to0)$ restricts to a short exact sequence on each $S$. Its Tate connection $a_S^j:T_S^j(\mathbb Z)\to T_S^{j+1}(I)$ is an isomorphism for every integer $j$. Under the degree-one comparison the image of $\mathbf 1_S$ is $[\alpha_S]$, where $\alpha_S(s)=e_s-e_1$.

*Proof.* The augmentation is equivariant because left translation permutes the basis and the scalar action is trivial. Its kernel is $I$, and $r e_1$ lifts $r$. Restriction changes the action and keeps the underlying maps, so injectivity, the kernel and surjectivity persist. As an $S$-module $P$ is a direct sum of copies of $\mathbb Z[S]$ indexed by the orbits $Sg$. By regular-module vanishing, $T_S^j(P)=0$ for every $j$. In the long exact sequence the two groups flanking $a_S^j$ are $T_S^j(P)$ and $T_S^{j+1}(P)$, which give injectivity and surjectivity. Lift the invariant $1$ to $e_1$. Its differential is $e_s-e_1$, which has augmentation zero and satisfies $\alpha_S(st)=\alpha_S(s)+s\alpha_S(t)$. The Tate differential out of degree zero agrees with the ordinary one under the cochain identifications. The incoming norm gives the quotient at degree zero and the outgoing differential gives exactly this one-cocycle. [Lean, read] `AugTrivial.lean:14–37,53–59,104–132`, `TateDeltaZero.lean:15–49`, `DimensionShift.lean:16–22`, `SplittingCohTrivial.lean:82–83`.

**Lemma 2 (the extension determined by the chosen class).** There is a short exact sequence $F_u=(0\to M\to B_u\to I\to0)$ produced as follows. Put $C=\operatorname{Map}(G,M)$, with $(g v)(h)=v(hg)$, and $j(m)(h)=hm$. Put $Q=C/j(M)$, with quotient $q$. Choose a one-cocycle $f:G\to Q$ whose ordinary connecting image for $0\to M\to C\to Q\to0$ is $u$. Define
$$
\beta\left(\sum_g a_g e_g\right)=\sum_g a_g f(g)\quad\text{on } I,\qquad
B_u=\{(v,x)\in C\oplus I:q(v)=\beta(x)\}.
$$
For every $S\le G$, the ordinary degree-one connection of $F_u|_S$ takes $[\alpha_S]$ to $u_S$.

*Proof.* Evaluation at $1$ is a left inverse of $j$ on underlying groups. The formula for $j$ is equivariant, since $(g j(m))(h)=hgm$. The coinduced middle term $C$ is Tate acyclic by the coinduction shift of §11.4.3.13. In particular $H^2(G,C)=0$, so the long exact sequence gives a surjection $H^1(G,Q)\to H^2(G,M)$. The cochain quotient gives a cocycle representative for a preimage. This is the choice made in `splittingCocycle`.

The cocycle rule gives $f(1)=0$ and $f(sg)=s f(g)+f(s)$. Thus on a sum with $\sum_g a_g=0$, the extra term $f(s)\sum_g a_g$ vanishes. This gives equivariance of $\beta$ and the identity $\beta(e_s-e_1)=f(s)$. The injection into $B_u$ takes $m$ to $(j(m),0)$ and is injective by the injection of $j$. The projection to $I$ is surjective because $q$ is surjective. If that projection kills $(v,x)$ then $x=0$ and $q(v)=0$, so $v=j(m)$ for a unique $m$. The module and the short exact sequence are preserved on restriction.

The first projection $B_u\to C$ gives a diagram of short exact sequences with identity on $M$ and $\beta$ on $I$. In that diagram the image of $\alpha_S$ under $\beta$ is $f|_S$ pointwise. For an explicit chase choose any lift $\widetilde f:G\to C$ of $f$. The lift of $\alpha_S$ to $B_u$ takes $s$ to $(\widetilde f(s),e_s-e_1)$. Its differential at $(s,t)$ is $((d\widetilde f)(s,t),0)$. The first coordinate has zero image in $Q$ because $df=0$. Applying $j^{-1}$ in the kernel therefore gives the restriction to $S$ of the very same two-cocycle that represents $u$. This is naturality first for the pullback diagram and then for ordinary cochain restriction. It proves the claimed equality with the specified class, with the positive connecting convention on both sequences. [Lean, read] `AugTrivial.lean:65–96`, `PullbackExtension.lean:14–95`, `SplittingModule.lean:20–100`, `DeltaMap.lean:12–38`.

**Theorem (conditional shift and zero-degree normalization).** For the finite group $G$, module $M$, and class $u$ above, let $b_S^i:T_S^i(I)\to T_S^{i+1}(M)$ be the Tate connection for $F_u|_S$. For every $S\le G$ the composite at degree zero, followed by the degree-two comparison, satisfies
$$
T_S^0(\mathbb Z)\xrightarrow{a_S^0}T_S^1(I)
 \xrightarrow{b_S^1}T_S^2(M)\simeq H^2(S,M),
\qquad \mathbf 1_S\longmapsto u_S.
$$
For any fixed $S$ and any $n\in\mathbb Z$, the composite $b_S^{n+1}a_S^n$ is an isomorphism if $T_S^{n+1}(B_u)=T_S^{n+2}(B_u)=0$. In particular it is an isomorphism in every degree for every $S$ under the following three hypotheses on **every** $T\le G$, including $T=G$:
$$
H^1(T,M)=0,\qquad
\operatorname{ord}(u_T)=|T|,\qquad
\forall v\in H^2(T,M)\ \exists m\in\mathbb Z\quad v=m u_T,
$$
by the [splitting-module application in §11.4.3.15](#114315-splitting-module-application), which proves the archive's `cohTrivial_splittingModule` under precisely these hypotheses. Here $\operatorname{ord}(0)=1$. The hypotheses are on the restriction of one specified $u$.

*Proof.* For $n=0$, Lemma 1 computes the first connection. For source degree one and target degree two the Tate and ordinary connections compare by taking the same lift and differential. Lemma 2 then computes the second map. A change from $1$ to $1+r|S|$ represents the same Tate class. The connection is defined on classes, and the computed output is killed by $|S|$ by linearity and the relation $|S|\mathbf 1_S=0$. In particular the normalization holds for the raw composite even when the hypotheses for invertibility are absent. At zero the composite is injective exactly when the order of $u_S$ is $|S|$, and surjective exactly when $u_S$ generates $H^2(S,M)$. This follows because the source is cyclic with generator $\mathbf 1_S$.

For arbitrary $n$, Lemma 1 makes the first map invertible. The second sits in the exact sequence
$$
T_S^{n+1}(B_u)\longrightarrow T_S^{n+1}(I)
 \xrightarrow{b_S^{n+1}} T_S^{n+2}(M)
 \longrightarrow T_S^{n+2}(B_u).
$$
The two stated vanishings suffice. More precisely this connection is invertible exactly when the two displayed outer arrows are zero, by exactness. The named archive theorem takes the three universally quantified hypotheses and makes $B_u$ cohomologically trivial, meaning all its Tate groups vanish on all subgroups. Its conclusion supplies these two zero groups at every $S,n$. The archive uses this theorem as an input at the second `asIso`. The final `eqToIso` only identifies $(n+1)+1$ with $n+2$. For the degree-zero Lean proof, `exists_tateComplex_cycles_zero` picks a cycle over $1$ and `tateCohomologyZeroIso_inv_mk` identifies its class with the input. The first lift is $e_1$, `tδ_comp_tateIsoH` compares the second connector, and `δ_splittingSES_res_δ₀` identifies its ordinary image. [Lean, read] `TateTheorem.lean:17–19,25–30,45–95`. [Lean, read] The proof of the vanishing used at the second `asIso` is the [splitting-module application in §11.4.3.15](#114315-splitting-module-application), `SplittingCohTrivial.lean:198–223`. It gives the two groups in degrees $n+1$ and $n+2$ on this same $S$.

**Hand calculation 1 (integers and $C_2$).** Write $G=\langle s\rangle=C_2$ and $M=\mathbb Z$ with trivial action. Let $c(i,j)=\lfloor(i+j)/2\rfloor$ for $0\le i,j<2$, the carry for multiplying $s^i,s^j$. More generally, for modulus $r$ the carry $c(i,j)$ is a cocycle: computing the total carry in $i+j+k$ using either association gives the same integer, and this is the trivial-action cocycle equation. Define $\widetilde f(s^i)(s^h)=c(h,i)$. Its differential is the constant function with value $c(i,j)$, by that equation. Hence $f=q\widetilde f$ is a cocycle with connecting image $[c]$. This choice is allowed in Lemma 2, and its image equals the one obtained from any choice for $u=[c]$.

In the cyclic model of [§11.4.3.11](#114311-cyclic-tate-periodicity-and-the-local-degree-two-order) the maps out of degrees zero and one are $D=s-1$ and $N=1+s$. The unit generates $\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z$. Its augmentation lift is $e_1$, so $D e_1=x=e_s-e_1$. In $I=\mathbb Z x$ the action is $s x=-x$. Thus the resulting class generates $\ker(N:I\to I)/D I=\mathbb Z x/2\mathbb Z x$. A lift of $x$ in $B_u$ is $X=(\widetilde f(s),x)$, with function coordinates $(0,1)$. The norm adds the two cyclic rotations, giving $NX=((1,1),0)=(j(1),0)$. The second connection therefore gives $[1]\in H^2(C_2,\mathbb Z)=\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z$. In ordinary cochains the same calculation gives the matrix with sole nonzero entry $c(1,1)=1$, where indices are exponents. For a normalized coboundary $d b$ its value there is $2b(s)$, so the carry is nonzero. The ambient $H^1$ vanishes because $2$ has trivial kernel on $\mathbb Z$, and the degree-two group is generated by this class of order two. On the identity subgroup the two positive groups vanish and the class has order one. This gives a nonzero instance with all the theorem's hypotheses.

**Hand calculation 2 (modulo three and $C_3$).** Keep the augmentation over $\mathbb Z$, and take $M=\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z$ with trivial action, as an integral representation. Use the carry with $r=3$, reduced modulo three, and the same $\widetilde f(s^i)(s^h)=c(h,i)$. The Tate input is still the integer unit in $\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z$. Write $x=e_s-e_1$ and $y=e_{s^2}-e_1$. The first connection sends the lift $e_1$ to $x$. On the basis $(x,y)$, $D x=y-2x$ and $D y=-x-y$, and $N I=0$. Thus in $I/D I$ we have $y=2x$ and $3x=0$. The homomorphism sending $x$ to $1$ and $y$ to $2$ modulo three kills $D I$ and shows that $[x]$ has order three.

Lift $x$ to $X=(\widetilde f(s),x)$, with function values $(0,0,1)$. The three translates sum to $NX=((1,1,1),0)=(j(1),0)$, which gives $[1]\in M/3M=H^2(C_3,M)$. In the bar model the degree-two representative is
$$
(c(i,j))_{i,j=0}^2=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\0&0&1\\0&1&1\end{pmatrix}.
$$
For any one-cochain $b$, $\sum_{i=0}^2(d b)(s^i,s)=3b(s)=0$ by telescoping. The same sum on the carry is $1$, proving nontriviality. Negating precisely one connecting map gives the class of $-c$, with sum $2$, so the result is a different class even though each signed map in this zero-degree calculation is still an isomorphism. Negating both connections gives the original composite. In the order-two calculation a single sign change is invisible. Here $H^1(C_3,M)=M$ since $D=N=0$ on $M$, and $T^1_{C_3}(\mathbb Z)=0$. Thus the zero-degree normalization survives in this example, while the shift at input degree one cannot be an isomorphism.

**A proper-subgroup failure.** Let $G=C_6=\langle s\rangle$ and $M=\mathbb Z\oplus A$, with $A=\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z$ and $s$ acting by $-1$ on $A$ and trivially on $\mathbb Z$. Take $u$ to be the integral carry for $r=6$ in the first summand. On $A$ the operator $s-1=-2$ is invertible and $N_G=0$. Thus the ambient $H^1(G,M)$ vanishes and $H^2(G,M)=\mathbb Z/6\mathbb Z$ is generated by $u$ of order six. The degree-two cyclic comparison evaluates a bar cocycle on $\sum_{i=0}^{5}[s^i|s]$. Its bar boundary is the norm applied to $[s]$, since the last two terms telescope. The carry evaluates to $1$, so the integral component generates the cyclic quotient modulo six. On $S=\langle s^2\rangle=C_3$ the action on $A$ is trivial, and restriction of the integral carry takes the values $\lfloor(2i+2j)/6\rfloor=\lfloor(i+j)/3\rfloor$. Consequently $u_S$ still has order three but generates only the first summand of $H^2(S,M)\simeq\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z\oplus\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z$, and $H^1(S,M)$ contains $A$. The normalized zero map cannot be onto this degree-two group. Testing only the ambient group therefore does not give the shift for every subgroup.

**Classical comparison and the next use.** Milne's Theorem II.3.11 assumes $G$ finite, integral group-module coefficients, $H^1(T,M)=0$, and $H^2(T,M)$ cyclic of order $|T|$ for every subgroup. His proof starts from a generator at $G$ and obtains generators on the subgroups by restriction and corestriction. The archive takes exact order and generation of the specified restrictions as explicit inputs. Milne constructs a splitting module via a two-cocycle and splices its extension with the augmentation on pp. 84–85. The archive constructs the extension by a coinduced quotient and pullback. The two proofs use regular-module vanishing and the all-subgroup vanishing of the splitting module to make the double connection invertible. Milne's Remark II.3.13 identifies it as cup product. [J. S. Milne, *Class Field Theory*, v4.03, II §3](https://www.jmilne.org/math/CourseNotes/CFT.pdf)

In the book's route, the inherited unit identity places $\mathbf 1_S$ in the left cup slot. The inherited two left-connection identities then turn $b_S^{n+1}a_S^n$ into left cup by the Tate class corresponding to $u_S$, with the target right unitor. Writing $v_S$ for the Tate degree-two class corresponding to $u_S$ and $\rho_M$ for the right unitor on the restricted coefficients, we obtain
$$
\Theta_S^n(x)=T_S^{n+2}(\rho_M)(v_S\smile x),
\qquad \Theta_S^n=b_S^{n+1}a_S^n.
$$
The first-slot degrees are $0$ and $1$, and the left-connection rule introduces no sign. Each output connector is transported through the right unitor using the short exact sequence tensored on the right by $\mathbb Z$. The finite consumer takes an inverse image for $n>0$ and uses ordinary comparison in bidegree $(2,n)$ and the order of the group to produce a torsion ordinary cohomology class. The existence of a class-formation fundamental class and verification of its subgroup hypotheses are supplied by the named [Lean] dependency `ClassFormation.groupFundamentalClass` and `ClassFormation.tate_hypotheses`. [Source ledger and consumer contract](../sources/SOURCES.md#chapter-11-114314-the-zero-degree-normalization-of-tates-isomorphism)

#### 11.4.3.15 Detecting cohomological triviality on subgroups

**Motivation.** In the splitting extension of [§11.4.3.14](#114314-the-zero-degree-normalization-of-tates-isomorphism), ordinary low-degree calculations give two zero groups on every subgroup. The connecting isomorphism needs two zero groups for each input degree, including negative degrees. Detection turns the low-degree calculation into this supply even when the coefficients have torsion.

**Examples.** The regular modules $(\mathbb Z/4\mathbb Z)[C_2]$ and $\mathbb F_2[C_2\times C_2]$ satisfy the detector. Trivial coefficients $\mathbb F_3$ for $S_3$ pass the two tests at the top while failing them on a Sylow subgroup.

**Definitions and theorem.** Let $G$ be finite, let $A$ be any left $\mathbb Z[G]$-module, and write $T_L^i(A)=\widehat H^i(L,A|_L)$ for $L\le G$ and $i\in\mathbb Z$. Call $A$ *Tate acyclic* on a given group when all its Tate groups there vanish. Call it *cohomologically trivial* when it is Tate acyclic on every subgroup. Fix $q\in\mathbb Z$. If $T_L^q(A)=T_L^{q+1}(A)=0$ for every $L\le G$, then $A$ is cohomologically trivial. In particular the ordinary conditions $H^1(L,A)=H^2(L,A)=0$ on every $L$ suffice by positive-degree comparison. The subgroup quantifier includes $G$ and $1$. The underlying abelian group is arbitrary. [Lean, read] CohTrivial.lean:119–126, CohTrivialBasic.lean:19–20, TateConnecting.lean:13–15. [Source record](../sources/SOURCES.md#chapter-11-114315-detecting-cohomological-triviality-on-subgroups)

**Lemma 1 (both dimension shifts).** For a finite group $P$ and module $X$, let $C(X)=\operatorname{Map}(P,X)$ with $(g f)(h)=f(hg)$ and embed $X$ by $x\mapsto(h\mapsto hx)$. Let $D(X)$ be the direct sum of copies of the underlying group of $X$ indexed by $P$, with left translation, and map $[g,x]\mapsto gx$ onto $X$. Evaluation at $1$ and the lift $[1,x]$ verify injectivity and surjectivity. Write $X^+$ for the first map's cokernel and $X^-$ for the second map's kernel. Restricting either short exact sequence keeps it exact. The middle terms restrict to regular permutation modules with underlying coefficients, by decomposing the indexing set into subgroup orbits. Regular permutation acyclicity and the two zeros flanking each connecting map give
$$
 T_L^i(X^+)\simeq T_L^{i+1}(X),\qquad
 T_L^i(X)\simeq T_L^{i+1}(X^-)
 \quad(L\le P,\ i\in\mathbb Z).
$$
If $pX=0$, the function and direct sum modules are killed by $p$, so the quotient $X^+$ and the submodule $X^-$ are as well. [Lean, read] DimensionShift.lean:16–22,34–54,79–98,104–118,142–166, CohTrivialBasic.lean:71–78,115–149, PGroupCohTrivialInt.lean:72–104. Regular permutation acyclicity is the supply used in §§11.4.3.12–13.

**Lemma 2 (the characteristic-$p$ base case).** Let $P$ be a finite $p$-group and $V$ an $\mathbb F_p[P]$-module. If $H_1(P,V)=0$, then $V$ is free over $\mathbb F_p[P]$.

*Proof.* Put $R=\mathbb F_p[P]$ and let $J$ be its augmentation ideal, so $V/JV$ is the group of coinvariants. Every nonzero $R$-module $W$ has a nonzero coinvariant. Choose a nonzero linear functional on $W$ and take the span of its finite orbit in the full dual. This is a nonzero finite vector space. The action of $P$ on its underlying set has orbit sizes powers of $p$, so the number of fixed points is divisible by $p$. Zero is fixed, hence there is a nonzero fixed functional. Such a functional kills $JW$ and descends to $W/JW$. Applying this argument to $W/U$ for a submodule $U$ shows that $W=U+JW$ implies $W=U$.

Lift a basis of $V/JV$ to $V$ and map the free $R$-module $F$ on those lifts to $V$. Its image plus $JV$ is all of $V$, so it surjects. It induces an isomorphism on coinvariants, and its kernel $K$ is contained in $JF$. For $x\in K$, choose a bar one-chain of $F$ whose boundary is $x$, since the image of the degree-one boundary is $JF$. The image of that chain in the bar chains of $V$ is a cycle, hence the boundary of a two-chain by $H_1(P,V)=0$. Lift the two-chain coefficientwise to $F$ and subtract its boundary from the original one-chain. The new one-chain has coefficients in $K$ and still has boundary $x$. Such a boundary is a sum of differences $g^{-1}y-y$ with $y\in K$, so $x\in JK$. Thus $K=JK$, and the coinvariant argument forces $K=0$. The construction allows an infinite basis. [Lean, read] PGroupFixed.lean:13–60,69–112, PGroupFree.lean:18–83,91–194.

For an integral module $X$ killed by $p$, passing to $\mathbb F_p[P]$ changes neither the additive bar chains in degrees zero through two nor their boundary formulas. Tate degree $-2$ is $H_1$, so $T_P^{-2}(X)=0$ makes $X$ free over $\mathbb F_p[P]$. In each regular summand take the coefficient projection onto the identity basis vector. Their direct sum $\phi$ satisfies $\sum_{g\in P}g\phi g^{-1}=\mathrm{id}$. For $P=\coprod_c Lc$, the endomorphism $\psi=\sum_c c\phi c^{-1}$ has $\sum_{\ell\in L}\ell\psi\ell^{-1}=\mathrm{id}$. This exhibits $X|_L$ as an equivariant retract of $\operatorname{Map}(L,X)$ by the inclusion in Lemma 1 and the retraction $f\mapsto\sum_{h\in L}h^{-1}\psi(f(h))$. Equivariance of the retraction follows by replacing $h$ by $hg$ in its sum, and the trace identity gives the identity composite. Therefore $X$ is cohomologically trivial with integral coefficients. [Lean, read] PGroupCohTrivialInt.lean:16–64,112–138, TateAddEquivAbelianizationTateClass.lean:29–30, CohTrivialBasic.lean:36–67,80–107, InducedNorm.lean:17–53.

For $pX=0$, one zero at any $q$ suffices. For $q>-2$ replace $(X,q)$ by $(X^+,q-1)$, and for $q<-2$ replace it by $(X^-,q+1)$. Lemma 1 preserves the vanishing and the coefficient condition. Induction on $|q+2|$ reaches the base case. The same isomorphisms, for every $L$ and every degree, propagate cohomological triviality back to the original module. [Lean, read] PGroupCohTrivial.lean:16–45, PGroupCohTrivialInt.lean:140–145.

**Lemma 3 (two zeros on a $p$-group).** For arbitrary integral coefficients $X$ on a finite $p$-group $P$, two adjacent Tate zeros on $P$ imply cohomological triviality.

*Proof.* First suppose multiplication by $p$ on $X$ is injective. In the sequence $0\to X\xrightarrow{p}X\to X/pX\to0$, the two given zeros surround $T_P^q(X/pX)$. The quotient is therefore cohomologically trivial by Lemma 2 and its shifts. Restrict the sequence to $L\le P$. The zero $T_L^{i-1}(X/pX)$ makes multiplication by $p$ on $T_L^i(X)$ injective, by exactness and additivity. On any finite group $L$, $|L|$ kills every Tate group. In degree zero, multiplying an invariant by $|L|$ gives its norm. For higher degrees use the cokernel shift in Lemma 1 to descend to degree zero, and for lower degrees use the kernel shift to ascend to zero. Since $|L|$ is a power of $p$, repeated injectivity gives $T_L^i(X)=0$.

For general $X$, map the free $\mathbb Z[P]$-module on the set of elements of $X$ onto $X$, sending the generator indexed by $x$ to $x$. Its kernel $K$ has injective multiplication by $p$ since the free module is torsion-free. The free middle term is acyclic on every subgroup, since its basis splits into regular orbits and the coefficient projection argument above applies. The connecting map identifies $T_L^i(X)$ with $T_L^{i+1}(K)$. Thus $K$ has two zeros at $q+1,q+2$ on $P$. The injective case proves cohomological triviality for $K$, and the same connecting maps prove it for $X$. [Lean, read] CohTrivialPGroup.lean:26–155, Annihilation.lean:14–55.

**Proof of the theorem.** Choose one Sylow $p$-subgroup of $G$ per prime $p$. The hypothesis at that subgroup gives acyclicity on all its subgroups by Lemma 3. Every $p$-subgroup of $G$ is conjugate to one of these smaller subgroups. For $U=gVg^{-1}$, the group map $u\mapsto g^{-1}ug$ and the coefficient map $a\mapsto g^{-1}a$ intertwine the actions. They relabel the bar complexes and preserve the norm. Likewise restriction along $L\hookrightarrow S\hookrightarrow G$ identifies the same action as restriction along the composite inclusion. These observations transport the vanishing to every $p$-subgroup of $G$.

For $L\le S$, restriction followed by corestriction acts on $T_S^i(A)$ as $[S:L]$. Here is the coefficient calculation. Restriction uses the coinduction unit $a\mapsto(h\mapsto ha)$ followed by Shapiro from coinduced to subgroup cohomology. Corestriction uses inverse Shapiro, then coinduction-to-induction and the induction counit. With the archive's inverse convention for induced symbols, the coefficient identification sends $f$ to $\sum_c[c,f(c)]$ over right cosets $Lc$ and the counit sends $[c,b]$ to $c^{-1}b$. Changing $c$ to $\ell c$ preserves the term by the induced relation and $f(\ell c)=\ell f(c)$. After Shapiro cancels, the coefficient composite is $a\mapsto\sum_c c^{-1}(ca)=[S:L]a$. Additivity gives the same multiple on Tate cohomology.

The comparison used here is all-degree Shapiro. Its positive cochain map comes from comparing the bar resolution of $L$ with the restricted bar resolution of $S$, which remains projective because the group ring restricts freely, and applying the coinduction adjunction. On the chain side use the inclusion of groups with the coefficient map extending $b$ to the function supported on $L$ with value $\ell b$ at $\ell\in L$. Its ambient norm is the constant function with value $N_L b$, also the cochain image of the invariant $N_L b$. Thus the two maps commute with the norm splice. The map is natural in the coefficients, so it commutes with the connecting homomorphisms. For descent from degree $j+1$ to $j$, use the kernel of a surjection onto the coefficient module from a regular function $L$-module and coinduce the sequence to $S$. The surjection sends $f$ to $\sum_h h^{-1}f(h)$ and admits a lift supported at the identity. Coinduction is exact by the product description over cosets, and the coinduced middle term is the regular function $S$-module by composition of coinductions. Both connecting maps are therefore isomorphisms, allowing the comparison for the kernel in degree $j+1$ to imply the comparison for the original module in degree $j$. Starting in degree one, induction with the coefficient module allowed to vary supplies every lower degree. [Lean, read] TateShapiro.lean:16–28,108–160,222–382,415–422, ResCores.lean:23–29,65–70, CoresRes.lean:32–54,66–78.

Now fix $S\le G$, $i\in\mathbb Z$, and $x\in T_S^i(A)$. Its order divides $|S|$ by the norm and shifts of Lemma 3. If its order exceeds one, choose a prime divisor $p$ and a Sylow $p$-subgroup $L$ of $S$. This $L$ is a $p$-subgroup of $G$, so its Tate group vanishes by the first paragraph. Consequently $[S:L]x=0$, contradicting the choice of $p$ since $p$ does not divide $[S:L]$. Hence all $T_S^i(A)$ vanish. The intermediate archive theorem allows the adjacent pair of degrees to depend on the prime and asks for the pair on only one Sylow subgroup of $G$ per prime. The conclusion on those Sylow groups includes their own subgroups, which is what the final application of transfer uses. [Lean, read] CohTrivial.lean:17–40,48–72,80–126, TateGroupIso.lean:19–91,121–142.

<a id="114315-splitting-module-application"></a>

**Application to the specified splitting module.** Retain the two exact sequences $0\to I_G\to\mathbb Z[G]\to\mathbb Z\to0$ and $0\to M\to B_u\to I_G\to0$ of [§11.4.3.14](#114314-the-zero-degree-normalization-of-tates-isomorphism), with $u\in H^2(G,M)$ and $u_S=\operatorname{res}^G_S u$. Suppose every $S\le G$ satisfies $H^1(S,M)=0$, $\operatorname{ord}(u_S)=|S|$, and $H^2(S,M)=\mathbb Zu_S$. In ordinary cohomology the augmentation connector onto $H^1(S,I_G)$ is surjective since $H^1(S,\mathbb Z[G])=0$. Its image of $1$ is the class $\alpha_S$ from [§11.4.3.14](#114314-the-zero-degree-normalization-of-tates-isomorphism). The invariant $\sum_{s\in S}e_s$ lifts $|S|$, so $|S|\alpha_S=0$. Also $H^2(S,I_G)=0$, since the connector from $H^1(S,\mathbb Z)=\operatorname{Hom}(S,\mathbb Z)=0$ is onto by regular acyclicity in degree two.

The splitting connector $d_S:H^1(S,I_G)\to H^2(S,M)$ sends $\alpha_S$ to $u_S$ by the inherited restricted-extension calculation. If $d_S(m\alpha_S)=0$, exact order gives $|S|\mid m$, hence $m\alpha_S=0$. The generation hypothesis makes $d_S$ onto. The exact segment
$$
 H^1(S,M)\longrightarrow H^1(S,B_u)\longrightarrow H^1(S,I_G)
 \xrightarrow{d_S} H^2(S,M)\longrightarrow H^2(S,B_u)\longrightarrow H^2(S,I_G)
$$
now gives $H^1(S,B_u)=H^2(S,B_u)=0$. On the left use injectivity of $d_S$ and $H^1(S,M)=0$. On the right use surjectivity of $d_S$ and $H^2(S,I_G)=0$. The positive comparisons turn these into the detector's hypotheses at $q=1$, so $B_u$ is cohomologically trivial. The $C_2$ carry example of [§11.4.3.14](#114314-the-zero-degree-normalization-of-tates-isomorphism) provides nonzero $M$ and a class satisfying all the hypotheses. In that example $B_u$ is itself nonzero since it surjects onto the rank-one augmentation ideal.

For input $n\in\mathbb Z$, the second connecting map in the restricted double boundary is $T_S^{n+1}(I_G)\to T_S^{n+2}(M)$. Its two flanking groups are $T_S^{n+1}(B_u)$ and $T_S^{n+2}(B_u)$. They vanish by the theorem. The first connecting map is invertible by the acyclicity of $\mathbb Z[G]|_S$ in degrees $n,n+1$. This supplies the two isomorphisms of TateTheorem.lean:45–53. [Lean, read] SplittingCohTrivial.lean:25–73,82–109,111–223. The class-formation caller supplies the chosen class and its three subgroup hypotheses through `ClassFormation.groupFundamentalClass` and `ClassFormation.tate_hypotheses` [Lean]. With these inputs, the all-integer isomorphism is the left cup by the specified restriction computed in [§11.4.3.14](#114314-the-zero-degree-normalization-of-tates-isomorphism).

**Hand calculation 1 (cyclic, with integral torsion).** Let $P=C_2=\langle s\rangle$ and $X=(\mathbb Z/4\mathbb Z)[P]$. Its coordinates are $(a,b)$ and $s(a,b)=(b,a)$. The periodic maps are
$$
 (s-1)(a,b)=(b-a,a-b),\qquad (1+s)(a,b)=(a+b,a+b).
$$
The kernel of $s-1$ is the diagonal and every diagonal element $(t,t)$ is $(1+s)(t,0)$. The kernel of $1+s$ consists of $(t,-t)$ and every such element is $(s-1)(0,t)$. Both Tate parities are zero, including the two positive tests in degrees one and two. On the trivial subgroup the norm is the identity. For the reduction step one can take the coordinatewise quotient of $\mathbb Z[P]$ by $4\mathbb Z[P]$ as a free cover, with kernel freely generated by $4e_1,4e_s$. Multiplication by two is injective on this kernel and its reduction modulo two is $\mathbb F_2[P]$ with the same swap. The elements $(1,0)$ in $X$ and $4e_1$ in the kernel witness that both objects are nonzero.

**Hand calculation 2 (noncyclic, in characteristic two).** Let $P=\{1,a,b,ab\}\simeq C_2\times C_2$ and $V=\mathbb F_2[P]$, with ordered coordinates $(w,x,y,z)$. The three involutions act by $(12)(34)$, $(13)(24)$ and $(14)(23)$. For each order-two subgroup the two pairs behave independently: the difference and norm agree in characteristic two and send $(r,s)$ to $(r+s,r+s)$. The kernel and image on each pair are the diagonal, so both periodic quotients are zero. For $P$ itself, the invariants are spanned by $(1,1,1,1)$ and $N_P(w,x,y,z)=(w+x+y+z)(1,1,1,1)$. The kernel of the norm is spanned by $e_1+e_a,e_1+e_b,e_1+e_{ab}$, all of which are coefficient differences, so Tate degrees zero and $-1$ give zero.

Here is also a calculation of the positive tests at $P$. Identify $V$ with $\operatorname{Map}(P,\mathbb F_2)$ by $e_g\mapsto\mathbf 1_{\{g^{-1}\}}$ and let $f$ be a homogeneous equivariant $n$-cochain. Evaluation at the identity identifies these cochains with arbitrary scalar functions $F$ on $P^{n+1}$: the inverse is $f(g_0,\ldots,g_n)(h)=F(hg_0,\ldots,hg_n)$. The differential is the alternating sum of omissions. The operator $sF(g_0,\ldots,g_{n-1})=F(1,g_0,\ldots,g_{n-1})$ satisfies $ds+sd=1$ for $n>0$. Indeed the omitted initial argument in $sd$ gives $F$, and each of the other omissions cancels its counterpart in $ds$. Thus each one- or two-cocycle is the differential of the displayed primitive $sF$. On the trivial subgroup the norm is the identity. This checks every subgroup and both tests on the nonzero module, witnessed by $e_1$. The coefficient projection at $1$ gives the trace identity directly on the four basis vectors, hence also proves all-degree vanishing without a cyclic-periodicity assumption on $P$.

**Boundary example (the top group alone).** Let $G=S_3$ and $A=\mathbb F_3$ with trivial action. With $L=\langle(123)\rangle$, the quotient $G/L$ is $C_2$. On $L$ both cyclic maps are zero, so $H^1(L,A)=H^2(L,A)=A$. A transposition acts on $L$ by inversion, hence acts by $-1$ on $H^1(L,A)=\operatorname{Hom}(L,A)$. It acts by $-1$ on $H^2(L,A)$ as well: for a central extension represented by a two-cocycle, the class in $A/3A$ is the cube of a chosen lift of $(123)$, written additively in the kernel. Replacing the generator by its inverse replaces this lift by its inverse, negating the cube. For the carry cocycle this cube is $1$, so this description identifies a generator and its negation. For completeness an extension here is determined by the lift $t$, the central kernel $A$, and $t^3\in A$, with replacements of $t$ changing $t^3$ by $3A$.

Restriction from $G$ is injective in degrees one and two because its composite with corestriction is multiplication by $2$, invertible on these $\mathbb F_3$-vector spaces. Its image consists of conjugation-invariant classes on $L$. In degree one this follows from the homomorphism description. In degree two, lift the transposition to the central extension of $G$; conjugating by that lift fixes the kernel and identifies the restricted extension with its transport by inversion. The invariant subspaces under negation are zero. Hence $H^1(G,A)=H^2(G,A)=0$ despite the two nonzero groups on $L$. The subgroup quantifier cannot be replaced by testing only $G$. The earlier $C_6$ example with sign action on $\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z$ in [§11.4.3.14](#114314-the-zero-degree-normalization-of-tates-isomorphism) also demonstrates failure, there at the level of the three hypotheses on the original coefficient module and its chosen class.

**Classical comparison.** Milne, *Class Field Theory*, v4.03, II Theorem 3.10, printed p. 83, assumes precisely a finite group, a $G$-module (an abelian group with action), and degrees one and two zero on all subgroups. His two degrees already use Tate notation in that section and agree with the ordinary groups. His conclusion is vanishing in all integer degrees at $G$. Apply it with $G$ replaced in turn by each subgroup to obtain the archive's conclusion. Neither proof requires coefficient finiteness. The archive additionally permits an arbitrary consecutive pair and the Sylow input stated above.

Milne uses cyclic periodicity as the base of an induction on the order of a solvable group. A nontrivial solvable group has a proper normal subgroup $N$ with cyclic quotient, by taking a cyclic prime-order quotient of its nontrivial abelianization. Induction gives all-degree acyclicity on $N$. For $r>0$, the inflation–restriction sequence of II Proposition 1.34 applies because $H^j(N,A)=0$ for $0<j<r$. Its injective inflation is also onto since $H^r(N,A)=0$. The two zeros at the top therefore give degrees one and two zero for the cyclic quotient with coefficients $A^N$. Periodicity gives every Tate degree there, and inflation gives all the positive zeros on the original group. In degree zero, quotient and subgroup norms are both onto the respective invariants, and $N_G=N_{G/N}\circ N_N$ on $A$. This gives the degree-zero zero at $G$.

For the negative degrees, tensor the augmentation sequence with the underlying abelian group of $A$ and use the diagonal action. Exactness persists because the augmentation is split over $\mathbb Z$. The middle term becomes a regular permutation module with underlying coefficients after untwisting by $e_g\otimes a\mapsto e_g\otimes g^{-1}a$. The kernel is the shift $A^-$ of Lemma 1. For each subgroup the two tests for the kernel are Tate degrees zero and one of $A$, which have just been proved on every subgroup by applying the nonnegative argument to that subgroup. Thus this kernel satisfies the same hypotheses. Iterating the kernel shift identifies the degree $-d$ group of $A$ with the degree-zero group after $d$ shifts. Finally, for arbitrary finite $G$, the solvable result applies to each Sylow subgroup, and restriction is injective on the corresponding primary component by the index identity of II Corollary 1.33 and the Tate version of that identity. Group-order annihilation then gives vanishing. This is Milne's solvable-group route. The pinned Lean proof replaces the solvable induction with the characteristic-$p$ argument of Lemmas 2–3. Milne's II Theorem 3.11, printed pp. 84–85, uses II.3.10 at the same point in the splitting-module argument. [Paper] [Milne's author PDF](https://www.jmilne.org/math/CourseNotes/CFT.pdf)

The three subgroup tests in this application are produced by the fundamental class in [§11.4.3.16](#114316-fundamental-classes-and-the-three-subgroup-hypotheses-in-a-class-formation).

#### 11.4.3.16 Fundamental classes and the three subgroup hypotheses in a class formation

**Motivation.** The finite-group machinery of §§11.4.3.13–11.4.3.14 and the [splitting-module application in §11.4.3.15](#114315-splitting-module-application) is conditional on four external inputs on each finite group $\Gamma$ and $\mathbb Z[\Gamma]$-module $M$: a distinguished cohomology class $u \in H^2(\Gamma, M)$ together with, for *every* subgroup $S \le \Gamma$, the vanishing $H^1(S, M) = 0$, the exact order $\operatorname{ord}(\operatorname{res}^\Gamma_S u) = |S|$, and the generation $H^2(S, M) = \mathbb Z\,\operatorname{res}^\Gamma_S u$. On a profinite Galois group $G$ acting on a discrete module $C$, these finite groups arise as open normal quotients $\Gamma = U/(V \cap U)$ with invariant coefficients $M = C^V$, and a *class formation* packages compatible invariant isomorphisms $\operatorname{inv}_U : H^2_{\mathrm{cts}}(U, C) \xrightarrow{\sim} \mathbb Q/\mathbb Z$ that scale by the subgroup index under restriction.
