**Lemma 3 (multiplicativity over the complete base).** Under the base hypotheses of the theorem, $s$ is multiplicative on any algebraic extension, and every power-multiplicative algebra norm extending $|\cdot|_K$ equals $s$.

*Proof.* Two such norms are equivalent on $K(x)$ by Lemma 1. Apply the comparison to $x^n$ and take $n$-th roots. Both comparison constants tend to $1$ after taking roots, so the norms agree at $x$. This works for each algebraic $x$.

Fix $c\ne0$. The sequence $s(yc^n)/s(c)^n$ decreases by submultiplicativity and is nonnegative. Let $A_c(y)$ be its limit. Homogeneity, the strong triangle inequality and $A_c(1)=1$ pass to the limit. The even subsequence and
$$
\frac{s(yzc^{2n})}{s(c)^{2n}}
\le \frac{s(yc^n)}{s(c)^n}\frac{s(zc^n)}{s(c)^n}
$$
give submultiplicativity, which also implies $A_c(y)A_c(y^{-1})\ge1$ for $y\ne0$. For a positive integer $m$, use the subsequence with indices $mn$ in $A_c(y^m)$ and power multiplicativity of $s$ to get $A_c(y^m)=A_c(y)^m$. Shifting the sequence by one gives $A_c(cy)=s(c)A_c(y)$. Since $A_c=s$ by uniqueness, $s(cy)=s(c)s(y)$. The case $c=0$ follows from $s(0)=0$. Every multiplicative absolute value extending the base is a power-multiplicative algebra norm, so uniqueness applies to it. [Pinned uniqueness and multiplication][ch11-ext-mul] · [Bosch–Güntzer–Remmert, §3.2.4][ch11-ext-bgr]

**Proof of the theorem.** The lemmas give the absolute value and the ultrametric. For $a,b\in K$, the identity $s(a-b)=|a-b|_K$ gives the isometry. The two norm bounds of Lemma 1 identify the spectral uniform structure with that of the finite product. Each Cauchy sequence converges in its coordinates, hence in the spectral metric.

Suppose the base is locally compact. A compact neighbourhood of zero contains a closed ball of positive radius, which is compact as a closed subset. By nontriviality and inversion choose $c\in K$ with $|c|_K>1$. Multiplication by $c^n$ produces compact base balls of arbitrarily large radii, so every closed base ball is compact. The lower coordinate estimate puts each closed spectral ball about zero inside a finite product of compact base balls. It is closed in that product, hence compact. Translation handles every centre. The spectral absolute value defines a valuation with the same comparison relation and strict balls as the metric construction. The isometric embedding retains a base element of norm strictly between zero and one, so the valuation remains nontrivial. Compatibility, local compactness and nontriviality are precisely the three fields of the archive's local-field class. [Pinned properness][ch11-ext-proper] · [Pinned local-field interface][ch11-ext-local]

**Agreement with the inherited valuation.** Let $v$ be a multiplicatively written valuation on an algebraic extension $\Omega/K$, with the original comparisons on $K$ and with a compatible topology. The bound $s(P)\le1$ for a monic polynomial is equivalent to every coefficient having base norm at most one. If $s(x)\le1$ and $v(x)>1$, the leading term of its minimal polynomial has strictly larger $v$-value than every other term, a contradiction. Thus $s(x)\le1$ implies $v(x)\le1$.

If $v(x)<1$ and $x\ne0$, then $s(x)\ge1$ would imply $s(x^{-1})\le1$, hence $v(x^{-1})\le1$, which is impossible. The case $x=0$ is immediate. Conversely, choose $a\in K$ with $0<|a|_K<1$. If $s(x)<1$, choose $n>0$ with $s(x)^n<|a|_K$. Apply the unit-ball implication to $x^n/a$ to obtain $v(x)^n\le v(a)<1$. We now have $v(x)<1$ exactly when $s(x)<1$. Comparing quotients gives all pairwise comparisons, with zero handled directly. In the image value group of $v$, the thresholds $v(a)^n$ are cofinal toward zero. Indeed for each $z\ne0$, eventually $|a|_K^n<s(z)$, giving $v(a)^n<v(z)$. These thresholds also correspond to a base of spectral balls at zero. Intersecting those balls with any intermediate field gives its subspace topology. Minimal polynomials commute with restriction, so on a finite intermediate field the induced topology and the topology constructed in the theorem agree. [A2 source map](../sources/SOURCES.md#chapter-11-finite-extensions-in-the-same-valuation-topology)

**Calculation 1 (unramified quadratic over $\mathbb Q_3$).** Put $L=\mathbb Q_3(i)$ with $i^2=-1$. A root in $\mathbb Q_3$ would have order zero and reduce to a root of $T^2+1$ in $\mathbb F_3$. The only squares there are $0,1$. Thus $(1,i)$ is a basis. Chapter 15 already computes the determinant $a^2+b^2$ of multiplication by $a+bi$ in this basis. Here is how that calculation gives the spectral value. For $b\ne0$ the minimal polynomial is $T^2-2aT+(a^2+b^2)$. With $v_3(0)=+\infty$ and $m=\min(v_3(a),v_3(b))$, the two coefficients $3^{-m}a$ and $3^{-m}b$ are integral and at least one is a unit. The sum of their squares is $1$ or $2$ modulo $3$. Therefore $v_3(a^2+b^2)=2m$ and $v_3(2a)\ge m$. The constant term attains the spectral maximum $3^{-m}$. When $b=0$ the linear minimal polynomial gives the same formula, with value zero at $a=b=0$.

Consequently the unit ball is $\mathbb Z_3+\mathbb Z_3 i$, with maximal ideal $3\mathbb Z_3+3\mathbb Z_3 i$. Coefficient reduction gives $\mathbb F_3[T]/(T^2+1)\cong\mathbb F_9$. The value $s(3)=1/3$ and the integral exponents $m$ show that $3$ is a uniformizer and $\operatorname{ord}_L(a+bi)=m$ on nonzero elements. The ramification index is $1$ and the residue degree is $2$. The residue-normalized absolute value of Chapter 15 is $9^{-m}$, whereas the extending value is $3^{-m}$. The nontrivial automorphism sends $i$ to $-i$. It induces the arithmetic Frobenius because $\bar i^3=-\bar i$, the same generator used in the unramified quadratic cocycle of §11.4.3.17. [Published determinant and order calculation](15-two-proofs-side-by-side.md#154313-the-canonical-discrete-value-group-of-a-local-field)

**Calculation 2 (Eisenstein quadratic over $\mathbb Q_5$).** Put $E=\mathbb Q_5(\pi)$ with $\pi^2=5$. The polynomial $T^2-5$ is Eisenstein. In degree two, irreducibility also follows since $2v_5(a)=1$ has no integer solution. In the basis $(1,\pi)$ the multiplication matrix for $a+b\pi$ has columns $(a,b)^\mathsf{T}$ and $(5b,a)^\mathsf{T}$. For $b\ne0$ the minimal polynomial is $T^2-2aT+(a^2-5b^2)$. Whenever both terms are nonzero, their orders $2v_5(a)$ and $1+2v_5(b)$ have different parity. Thus
$$
m=\min(2v_5(a),1+2v_5(b))=v_5(a^2-5b^2),\qquad
s(a+b\pi)=5^{-m/2}.
$$
Indeed $v_5(2a)=v_5(a)\ge m/2$, so the square root of the constant-term absolute value attains the spectral maximum. The zero coefficient convention includes $a=0$, and the linear case covers $b=0$, giving $s(0)=0$.

The unit ball is $\mathbb Z_5+\mathbb Z_5\pi$. The strict unit ball is $5\mathbb Z_5+\mathbb Z_5\pi=\pi(\mathbb Z_5+\mathbb Z_5\pi)$, and reduction $a+b\pi\mapsto\bar a$ gives $\mathbb F_5$. Hence $\pi$ is a uniformizer with $s(\pi)=5^{-1/2}$, $\operatorname{ord}_E(\pi)=1$, and $\operatorname{ord}_E(a+b\pi)=m$ for nonzero elements. Here $e=2$ and $f=1$. On $\mathbb Q_5^\times$, $\operatorname{ord}_E=2v_5$ while $s=|\cdot|_5$. The residue-normalized absolute value is $5^{-m}$. This is the degree-two ramified field in the tower of §11.4.3.17, with $\pi=\varpi^2$ when $\varpi^4=5$.

In both examples the maximal ideal is determined before any Haar measure is chosen. The discrete order gives the balls $\pi^n\mathcal O$ in the notation of [§15.4.3.13](15-two-proofs-side-by-side.md#154313-the-canonical-discrete-value-group-of-a-local-field). The coset count of [§15.4.3.11](15-two-proofs-side-by-side.md#154311-haar-measure-on-p-adic-balls-and-shells) applies with the uniformizer of the extension and $q=9$ or $q=5$. Indeed, each ball has $q$ subballs at the next integer order by reduction, so with additive Haar mass one on $\mathcal O$, the order-$n$ ball has mass $q^{-n}$ and the shell has mass $(1-q^{-1})q^{-n}$. The extending absolute value gives the radii $3^{-n}$ and $5^{-n/2}$ of those same balls. The additive volume factor is given by the residue-normalized value in these examples. [Sutherland, Theorem 10.4 and Remark 10.7][ch11-ext-suth]

**Why finite dimension matters.** Let $K=\mathbb F_3((t))$ and $F_j=\mathbb F_3((t^{1/2^j}))$ for $j\ge0$, using compatible roots. Each $F_j$ is a finite separable extension of $K$. Grouping exponents by their residues modulo $2^j$ gives a basis $1,t^{1/2^j},\ldots,t^{(2^j-1)/2^j}$, and the generator satisfies the separable polynomial $T^{2^j}-t$. Give $F=\bigcup_{j\ge0}F_j$ the least-exponent valuation $w$, with $|x|=3^{-w(x)}$ and $|0|=0$. This is the extending absolute value by Lemma 3. The value group is $\mathbb Z[1/2]$. The points $t^{1/2^j}$ for $j\ge1$ lie in the unit ball and any two are at distance at least $3^{-1/2}$ by their unequal orders. Every metric neighbourhood of zero contains a scalar multiple by some $t^m$ of this separated set. Therefore no compact neighbourhood of zero exists.

The partial sums $s_N=\sum_{j=1}^N t^{j+2^{-j}}$ are Cauchy since the exponents increase to infinity. If $y\in F_M$ were a limit, for $N>M$ the least exponent in $s_N-y$ would be at most $M+1+2^{-(M+1)}$. The coefficient at that exponent cannot cancel, since its reduced denominator is $2^{M+1}$ and every exponent of $y$ lies in $2^{-M}\mathbb Z$. The bound prevents convergence to $y$. Thus this algebraic separable union is neither complete nor locally compact. A separable closure containing it also contains the same separated set in every sufficiently scaled neighbourhood, so local compactness fails there as well. The completeness argument here is for the displayed union. [Classical algebraic-extension boundary, Milne, Remark 7.41][ch11-ext-milne]

**Return to the class formation.** The archive chooses the base norm on the original additive uniform space and installs the spectral norm on the extension. At the finite stage it uses that norm both to make the valuative relation and to make the topology. For an algebraic ambient extension, the inherited comparison and neighbourhood proofs identify those two finite-stage choices with the ambient restriction. In the separable closure this applies to every open subgroup's fixed field $K_U$, using the finite-dimensionality supplied by the open-subgroup correspondence. The local invariant in §11.4.3.17 uses that local-field instance at $K_U$. When the archive starts from a bare separable closure, it installs the algebraic valuative relation and its compatible topology first. The cohomological coefficient group in the class formation is given its discrete action as described in §11.4.3.17. [Archive declarations and direct consumers](../sources/SOURCES.md#chapter-11-finite-extensions-in-the-same-valuation-topology)

**Relation to the earlier completions.** [§5.3.1.17](05-the-square-root-barrier-core-gm.md#53117-finite-place-completion-embeddings-and-tensor-norm-transport) constructs the completion map at a prescribed place and carries a tensor norm witness along it. Here the given base field is already complete, and the spectral formula constructs the metric on a finite algebraic extension. [§8.4.13](08-divisibility-at-inert-primes.md#8413-the-normalized-local-norm-supply-nm_restnorm_of_place) explains the separate completion used before extending the place norm to an algebraic closure. Lemmas 2–3 explain the cited spectral declaration there. The finite-dimensional conclusion applies to its finite layers.

**Reading status.** [Lean, read] The source ledger gives the proof-body ranges for the archive's finite local-field construction, the pinned Mathlib norm extension, and the actual intermediate-field consumer. The classical proof and computations above are the book's exposition of those ingredients. The next ramification, higher ramification and local Tate duality producers keep their separate reading status. #### 11.4.3.22 Congruence kernels and inertia in the relative cyclotomic tower

**Motivation.** Proposition 6 in §11.4.3.2 needs one automorphism to test whether a class character is invariant under the relative Galois group. It also needs to know which absolute inertia groups act trivially on the finite layer. Both questions reduce to how automorphisms act on roots of unity. The group fixing the base field and the group fixing the residue field arise from different restrictions.

**Example 1 (conductor $15$ to conductor $135$).** Take $f=5$, $\ell=3$, $e=1$ and $n=3$. The units modulo $135$ reducing to $1$ modulo $15$ are precisely
$$
1,\ 16,\ 31,\ 46,\ 61,\ 76,\ 91,\ 106,\ 121 .
$$
These are the representatives $1+15j$ for $0\le j<9$. Each is $1$ modulo both $3$ and $5$. The target units are $1,2,4,7,8,11,13,14$ modulo $15$. Each has nine lifts, all units, giving $72$ units modulo $135$ and a kernel of order $72/8=9$.

The table computes the full power cycle of $\gamma=16$. Each row gives the multiplication and reduction that advances it.

| $j$ | $16^j\bmod 135$ | $16^{j+1}\bmod 135$ by subtraction |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| $0$ | $1$ | $16$ |
| $1$ | $16$ | $256-135=121$ |
| $2$ | $121$ | $1936-14\cdot135=46$ |
| $3$ | $46$ | $736-5\cdot135=61$ |
| $4$ | $61$ | $976-7\cdot135=31$ |
| $5$ | $31$ | $496-3\cdot135=91$ |
| $6$ | $91$ | $1456-10\cdot135=106$ |
| $7$ | $106$ | $1696-12\cdot135=76$ |
| $8$ | $76$ | $1216-9\cdot135=1$ |

The nine entries are distinct and exhaust the kernel. For a primitive $135$th root $\zeta$, the generator acts by $\zeta\mapsto\zeta^{16}$ and fixes $\zeta^9$, which has order $15$. Its inverse acts by $\zeta\mapsto\zeta^{76}$, as $16\cdot76\equiv1\pmod{135}$. The lemmas below show that at each prime above $3$ the inertia in $\mathbb Q(\zeta_{135})/\mathbb Q(\zeta_{15})$ has order $9$ and is generated by either automorphism. At every other finite prime, including primes over $5$, the relative inertia is trivial.

**Example 2 (starting the binary tower at $e=1$).** Take $f=1$, $\ell=2$, $e=1$ and $n=3$. The units modulo $8$ are $\{1,3,5,7\}$ and all reduce to $1$ modulo $2$. The kernel has order $4$. Directly,
$$
3^2=9\equiv1,\qquad 5^2=25\equiv1,\qquad
7^2=49\equiv1,\qquad 3\cdot5\equiv7\pmod 8 .
$$
The elements $3$ and $5$ generate $C_2\times C_2$. The proposed $\gamma=3$ generates only $\{1,3\}$. The base field is $\mathbb Q(\zeta_2)=\mathbb Q$, and at $2$ inertia is the whole noncyclic group by Lemma 4 below. With $e=2$, the target units are $\{1,3\}$ modulo $4$. The residues $1,5$ map to $1$ and $3,7$ map to $3$. The kernel is $\{1,5\}$, generated by $1+4=5$, and gives the inertia in $\mathbb Q(\zeta_8)/\mathbb Q(i)$ at $2$.

**Definitions.** Set $m=f\ell^e$, $M=f\ell^n$ and $U_d=(\mathbb Z/d\mathbb Z)^\times$. When $m$ divides $M$, reduction gives $r_{M,m}\colon U_M\to U_m$. Write $H_{M,m}=\ker r_{M,m}$, the subgroup of classes congruent to $1$ modulo $m$. Write $\sigma_t$ for the automorphism of $\mathbb Q(\zeta_M)$ sending $\zeta_M$ to $\zeta_M^t$. For a prime $\mathfrak P$ of $L$ above $\mathfrak p$ of $F$, the *decomposition group* is the subgroup of $\operatorname{Gal}(L/F)$ stabilizing $\mathfrak P$. The *inertia group* $I(\mathfrak P/\mathfrak p)$ is the kernel of its action on $\mathcal O_L/\mathfrak P$.

**Lemma 1 (restriction of cyclotomic automorphisms).** [Lean, read] Suppose $M>0$, $m\mid M$ and $\gcd(f,\ell)=1$. Given number fields $F$ and $L$ cyclotomic over $\mathbb Q$ of levels $m$ and $M$, respectively, there is an embedding $F\hookrightarrow L$ over which $L=F(\zeta_{\ell^n})$. With this embedding,
$$
H_{M,m}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\operatorname{Gal}(L/F),
\qquad t\longmapsto\sigma_t .
$$

**Proof.** Write $M=km$. If $\zeta$ has order $M$, then $\zeta^k$ has order $m$. A primitive $m$th root in $F$ and $\zeta^k$ have the same minimal polynomial $\Phi_m$ over $\mathbb Q$, which gives the embedding taking the former to the latter. Cancelling $f$ gives $\ell^n=k\ell^e$, so both $\zeta^{\ell^n}=(\zeta^k)^{\ell^e}$ and $\zeta^f$ lie in $F(\zeta^f)$. Since $f$ and $\ell^n$ are coprime, Bézout's identity recovers $\zeta$ from these two powers. The power $\zeta^f$ has order $\ell^n$.

Over $\mathbb Q$, every automorphism is a unique $\sigma_t$. Irreducibility of $\Phi_M$ lets us send $\zeta$ to any primitive $M$th root, and $\zeta$ generates the field. Composition multiplies the exponents. On $\zeta^k$ this action raises to the same exponent $t$, measured modulo $m$. Thus restriction is reduction, and fixing $\zeta^k$ is equivalent to $t\equiv1\pmod m$. $\square$

`relCyc` quantifies over natural numbers $\ell,f,e,n$ with a nonzero-modulus instance and an explicit divisibility witness. It constructs an algebra structure on the two given fields and states the correspondence as two existence assertions with equality of underlying ring equivalences. Its restriction helper reduces exponents on the image of the lower primitive root and extends the equality using the power basis. For generation it uses that $\zeta^{f+\ell^n}$ is primitive and its powers include $\zeta$. The proof above uses Bézout for that step.

**Lemma 2 (order and generator of the reduction kernel).** Let $\ell$ be prime, $f\ge1$ coprime to $\ell$, and $n\ge e\ge1$. Then $\lvert H_{M,m}\rvert=\ell^{n-e}$. If $\ell$ is odd or $e\ge2$, then
$$
H_{M,m}=\langle\gamma\rangle,\qquad
\gamma=1+f\ell^e\bmod M,\qquad \operatorname{ord}(\gamma)=\ell^{n-e}.
$$

**Proof.** The moduli have the same prime divisors. Every unit modulo $m$ has exactly $M/m$ lifts, all units. In particular reduction is onto, and its kernel consists of the $\ell^{n-e}$ distinct classes of $1+jm$ for $0\le j<\ell^{n-e}$. The same count by the Chinese remainder theorem and the prime-power totient is
$$
\lvert H_{M,m}\rvert
=\frac{\varphi(f)\ell^{n-1}(\ell-1)}
       {\varphi(f)\ell^{e-1}(\ell-1)}
=\ell^{n-e}.
$$

Let $x=1+f\ell^e$ as an integer. Because $x\equiv1\pmod f$, the condition $\gamma^a=1$ for any positive integer $a$ is equivalent to $\ell^n\mid x^a-1$. For $j\ge0$ we claim
$$
v_\ell(x^{\ell^j}-1)=e+j ,
$$
where $v_\ell$ is the exponent of $\ell$ in a nonzero integer. The case $j=0$ follows from $\ell\nmid f$. For odd $\ell$, if $y=1+\ell^s u$ with $s\ge1$ and $\ell\nmid u$, the linear term of $y^\ell-1$ is $\ell^{s+1}u$. The terms of degree $2$ through $\ell-1$ are divisible by $\ell^{2s+1}$ and the last term by $\ell^{s\ell}$. Both exponents are at least $s+2$, so $v_\ell(y^\ell-1)=s+1$. For $\ell=2$ and $s\ge2$, $y+1=2(1+2^{s-1}u)$ has valuation $1$. Thus $v_2((y-1)(y+1))=s+1$. Induction gives the claim.

For $d=n-e>0$ we have $\gamma^{\ell^d}=1$ and $\gamma^{\ell^{d-1}}\ne1$. The order divides $\ell^d$ and is therefore exactly $\ell^d$. For $d=0$ the kernel is trivial. Since $\gamma$ belongs to the kernel and has its full order, it generates it. $\square$

[Lean, read] `gamGen` fixes $e=\operatorname{ee}(\ell)$, equal to $1$ for odd primes and $2$ for $\ell=2$ (`TwSingle.lean:7`). It takes a prime instance, the divisibility witness, a unit $\gamma$, coprimality, $e\le n$ and the residue equality. The equality with the subgroup of integer powers and the equation $\gamma^{\ell^n}=1$ are its two outputs. The sharper order is established inside the proof. The archive uses the odd and binary lifting-the-exponent lemmas and the totient calculation. The elementary calculation above also covers odd $\ell$ with arbitrary $e\ge1$. The bound $e\le2\le n$ in Proposition 6 comes from $\operatorname{ee}$ and $n\ge2$ at the call site, `AmbCyc.lean:38–42`.

**Lemma 3 (absolute inertia away from $\ell$).** [Lean, read] Let $F\subseteq K\subseteq\Omega$ be as in `ineCyc`, with $F,K$ number fields and $\Omega$ an algebraic closure of both fields with compatible embeddings. Let $\ell$ be prime, $n$ any natural number and $K/F$ cyclotomic of level $\ell^n$. For any valuation subring $A$ of $\Omega$ in which $\ell$ is a unit, the absolute inertia $\operatorname{Ine}(A)$ in $\operatorname{Gal}(\Omega/F)$ is contained in $\operatorname{Gal}(\Omega/K)$.

**Proof.** In multiplicative valuation notation the hypothesis is $\neg(v_A(\ell)<1)$. Since $\ell$ belongs to $A$, this means $v_A(\ell)=1$. An element $g$ of $\operatorname{Ine}(A)$ stabilizes $A$ and satisfies $v_A(gz-z)<1$ for $z\in A$. A root $\zeta$ of $X^{\ell^n}-1$ has $v_A(\zeta)=1$ since the value group is torsion-free. Thus $\eta=g\zeta/\zeta$ satisfies $\eta^{\ell^n}=1$ and reduces to $1$ modulo the maximal ideal of $A$.

If $\eta^b=1$ for a positive integer $b$ that is a unit in $A$, then reduction to $1$ forces $\eta=1$. Indeed, if $\eta\ne1$, cancellation in the geometric-sum identity gives $\sum_{i=0}^{b-1}\eta^i=0$. Each term reduces to $1$, forcing $b=0$ in the residue field, a contradiction. This is the argument of `iC_one`, `iC_pow` and `iC_eta` in valuation notation. Applying it with $b=\ell^n$ shows that $g$ fixes all the generating roots. The proof ends by induction on the algebra they generate over $F$. $\square$

**Lemma 4 (finite relative inertia).** In the situation of Lemma 2 and for the fields of Lemma 1, under the identification with $H_{M,m}$,
$$
I(\mathfrak P/\mathfrak p)=
\begin{cases}
H_{M,m},&\mathfrak P\mid\ell,\\
\{1\},&\mathfrak P\nmid\ell .
\end{cases}
$$
In the cyclic range of Lemma 2 the first group is generated by $\sigma_\gamma$ and the second by the identity. The equality of groups includes Example 2 at $e=1$.

**Proof.** Use the classical cyclotomic integral basis $\mathcal O_L=\mathbb Z[\zeta_M]$. Put $\xi=\zeta_M^f$ and $\omega=\zeta_M^{\ell^n}$. The same Bézout identity as in Lemma 1 gives $\mathcal O_L=\mathbb Z[\omega,\xi]$, since negative powers of roots of unity are also nonnegative powers. Also $\omega\in F$. If $\mathfrak P\mid\ell$, the only root of $X^{\ell^n}-1$ in the residue field is $1$, since in characteristic $\ell$ this polynomial is $(X-1)^{\ell^n}$. For every $\sigma_t$ in the relative group, $\sigma_t(\xi)=\xi^t$ has the same reduction as $\xi$, and $\sigma_t$ fixes $\omega$. Thus $z\mapsto\overline{\sigma_t(z)}$ and $z\mapsto\bar z$ agree on the ring of integers. Their kernels agree, so $\sigma_t$ stabilizes $\mathfrak P$ and acts trivially on its residue field.

For residue characteristic different from $\ell$, an inertia element satisfies $\sigma_t(\xi)/\xi\equiv1\pmod{\mathfrak P}$. The geometric-sum argument in the localization at $\mathfrak P$ forces $\sigma_t(\xi)=\xi$. As it fixes $F$ and $\xi$, it is the identity. $\square$

**Remarks.** The direct factor in Remark 4 of §11.4.3.2 is specific to the archive's choice of $e$. By the Chinese remainder theorem the factor $U_f$ is common to both moduli. For odd $\ell$ and $e=1$, reduction on the $\ell$-power coordinate has kernel of order $\ell^{n-1}$ and quotient of order $\ell-1$. The prime-to-$\ell$ subgroup of the finite abelian group on that coordinate maps isomorphically onto the quotient. For $\ell=2$ and $e=2$, the two classes $\{\pm1\}$ give a section of reduction modulo $4$. In both cases complex conjugation has nontrivial image on the lower $\ell$-power coordinate.

Lemma 4 is a textbook deduction from the cyclotomic integral basis and the action on residue fields. Proposition 6 uses the absolute containment of Lemma 3 at `AmbIndex.lean:39` and the generator $\tau=\sigma_{\gamma^{-1}}$ from `AmbCyc.lean:45–73`. With the chapter's convention $[t]\mapsto\sigma_t^{-1}$, it is $\tau$ that acts as $[\gamma]$ on the class group.

In Example 2 the binary valuation step fails at $e=1$. For $y=3$ one has $v_2(y+1)=2$, so $v_2(y^2-1)=3$. Coprimality in Lemma 1 also matters. If $f=\ell=3$, $e=1$ and $n=3$, the levels are $9$ and $81$, but $\mathbb Q(\zeta_9)(\zeta_{27})=\mathbb Q(\zeta_{27})$ has degree $18$ over $\mathbb Q$, whereas $\mathbb Q(\zeta_{81})$ has degree $54$. Adjoining the indicated prime-power root gives only a proper subfield.

Here `GL/` denotes `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/AutomorphicVarieties/GaloisLibrary/`. The source ledger locates the Mathlib unit, cyclotomic and inertia interfaces and the inherited inputs. The cyclotomic integral basis is a classical input to Lemma 4. The declarations `finChar`, `pullEq` and `pullMem` retain their named [Lean] roles in Proposition 6. ### 11.4.5 From two zero annihilators to a perfect local cup pairing

**Motivation.** The [cyclic norm criterion and its Kummer comparison](#114318-local-cup-symbols-and-evaluation-of-artin-reciprocity) explain how a local cup class vanishes. Its two annihilator consequences tell us that each nonzero class is detected by some class on the other side. A perfect pairing gives a stronger answer. Every character on either side must be evaluation against a class on the other side, and that class must be unique. The proof starts with the finite quotient of field units by powers, uses Kummer theory to bound one cohomology group, and closes a cycle of four inequalities. This is the degree-$(1,1)$ root-of-unity input to the local duality package. [Lean, read] A2, `LocalTateDuality/EqZeroOfForallCupPairingZmodMulEqZeroLeft.lean:38–66,117–149`. [Source ledger](../sources/SOURCES.md#chapter-11-finite-perfect-local-cup-pairing)

**Two examples to keep in view.** Over $\mathbb Q_3$, the square classes are generated by $3$ and $-1$. Two quadratic characters distinguish these coordinates. Over $\mathbb Q_5$, the tenth-power quotient has a valuation coordinate of order $10$, a residue-unit coordinate of order $2$, and a principal-unit coordinate of order $5$. The last coordinate pairs with a wild character. A perfect matrix in the second example tests characters on cyclic groups of different orders.

**The objects.** Let $K$ be a non-archimedean local field, $\Omega$ a chosen separable closure, $G_K=\operatorname{Gal}(\Omega/K)$ with the Krull topology, and $U\le G_K$ any open subgroup. Put $F=\Omega^U$. Galois correspondence and the local-field structure on finite separable extensions make $F$ a non-archimedean local field. Restricting scalars identifies $G_F$ topologically with $U$ inside $G_K$, with its given action on the very same $\Omega$. This works for nonnormal $U$. Let $n$ be a natural number satisfying $(n:K)\ne0$. The injection $K\hookrightarrow F$ gives $(n:F)\ne0$, and $n\ge1$.

For this $U$ set
$$
X=H^1_{\mathrm{cts}}(U,\mathbb Z/n),\qquad
Y=H^1_{\mathrm{cts}}(U,\mu_n(\Omega)),\qquad
Z=H^2_{\mathrm{cts}}(U,\mu_n(\Omega)).
$$
The first action is trivial and the root module carries its restricted Galois action. Cohomology uses discrete coefficients. The coefficient pairing is $(j,\zeta)\mapsto\zeta^j$. It gives the cup product $\beta:X\times Y\to Z$. Here $X=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(U,\mathbb Z/n)$, since for the trivial action the cocycle equation is the homomorphism equation and the degree-one coboundaries are zero. The roots of $T^n-1$ in $\Omega$ form a cyclic group of order $n$, since the polynomial is separable and splits in $\Omega$. The resulting abstract cyclic group identification does not trivialize its action.

Let $t=\operatorname{localInvRootsOfUnity}_{K,\Omega,n,U}:Z\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb Z/n$, and let $j_n:\mathbb Z/n\hookrightarrow\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z$ send $j$ to $[j/n]$. The defining normalization is $j_n(t(c))=\operatorname{inv}_F(i(c))$, where $i$ is the Kummer degree-two inclusion into $H^2_{\mathrm{cts}}(U,\Omega^\times)$. Indeed, Hilbert 90 makes $i$ injective, exactness identifies its image with the kernel of multiplication by $n$, and the local Brauer invariant identifies this kernel with $(\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z)[n]$. The archive identifies the two ranges and inverts $j_n$ on that common range. The local invariant and Hilbert 90 enter here as named inputs. [Lean, read] `LocalTateDuality/InjectiveCohomologyMapKummerSeqOpenFTwo.lean:82–93,137–219`.

We use the two established annihilator statements for this exact cup. For each $x\in X$, if $\beta(x,y)=0$ for all $y\in Y$, then $x=0$. For each $y\in Y$, if $\beta(x,y)=0$ for all $x\in X$, then $y=0$. They use norm detection and norm separation through the cyclic Kummer cup criterion. Their common field is $F$ and every norm test is in the fixed field of the corresponding character kernel in $\Omega$. [Lean] `LocalTateDuality/EqZeroOfForallCupPairingZmodMulEqZeroLeft.lean:68–115`, with `LocalTateDuality/NormCharacters.lean:38–139` and `LocalTateDuality/KummerCup.lean:225–252`.

**Lemma 1 (the supply of finite power quotients).** Let $F$ be a non-archimedean local field and $(n:F)\ne0$. Write $v$ for the additive valuation with $v(\pi)=1$, $\mathcal O$ for the valuation ring, $\mathfrak m=(\pi)$, $k=\mathcal O/\mathfrak m$, and $q=|k|$. Put $V_0=\mathcal O^\times$ and $V_j=1+\mathfrak m^j$ for $j\ge1$. Then $P=(F^\times)^n$ is open and $F^\times/P$ is finite. Explicitly, with $d=v(n)$ and $N=2d+1$, there is a surjection
$$
\{0,\ldots,n-1\}\times (\mathcal O^\times/V_N)
 \longrightarrow F^\times/P,\qquad (r,\bar u)\longmapsto[\pi^r u],
$$
and $[\mathcal O^\times:V_N]=(q-1)q^{N-1}$.

*Proof.* Reduction $\mathcal O^\times\to k^\times$ is onto, since any lift of a nonzero residue is a unit. Its kernel is $V_1$. At each $j\ge1$, write a member of $V_j$ uniquely as $1+\pi^j a$. The map to $\bar a\in(k,+)$ is additive under multiplication, since the product has coefficient $a+b+\pi^jab$. Its kernel is $V_{j+1}$, and each residue has a lift $a$ for which $1+\pi^j a$ is a unit. Thus the successive indices are $q$. Multiplying indices gives the formula, including $N=1$. These are the residue map, graded coefficient map and finite-index instance of `LocalFields/UnitsBasic.lean:275–293,306–374,376–410`. [Lean, read] [Serre, *Local Fields*, IV §2, Proposition 6, pp. 66–67](https://www.sas.rochester.edu/mth/sites/doug-ravenel/otherpapers/Serre-Local.pdf)

Write $n=\pi^d\epsilon$ in $\mathcal O$ with $\epsilon$ a unit and let $w\in V_N$. For $f(T)=T^n-w$, Taylor expansion at $1$ gives
$$
f(1+\pi^{d+1}T)=\pi^{2d+1}\bigl(c+\epsilon T+\pi T^2 h(T)\bigr),
\qquad c=(1-w)/\pi^{2d+1},\qquad h(T)\in\mathcal O[T].
$$
The linear term has valuation factor $\pi^{2d+1}$ and every term of degree at least two has factor $\pi^{2d+2}$. The polynomial in parentheses, say $g$, has residue root $-\bar c/\bar\epsilon$ with nonzero derivative. To see the completeness step used by the archive, start at any lift $a_0$ of this root. Set $a_{i+1}=a_i-g(a_i)/g'(a_i)$. Inductively $g'(a_i)$ is a unit, $a_{i+1}\equiv a_i\bmod\mathfrak m$, and $g(a_i)\in\mathfrak m^{i+1}$. The polynomial identity for $g(a_i+h)$ cancels the constant and linear terms for this choice of $h$ and makes the new error divisible by $\mathfrak m^{2(i+1)}$. The sequence converges in the complete ring and its limit satisfies $g=0$ since the ring is separated and polynomial evaluation is continuous. Then $1+\pi^{d+1}\lim_i a_i$ is a unit whose $n$-th power is $w$. This proves $V_N\subset P$ including $d>0$. The formal root supplier performs the same rescaling and uses the compatible residues of the Newton sequence. [Lean, read] `LocalFields/UnitsPowers.lean:12–15,28–31,156–182` and `Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/IntegralCasts/CapelliIdentities/ExistsRootOfAdicComplete.lean:7–84,86–130,134–152`. [Milne, *Algebraic Number Theory*, §7, Proposition 7.31 and Theorem 7.32, pp. 119–121](https://www.jmilne.org/math/CourseNotes/ANT.pdf)

The image of $V_N$ in $F^\times$ is the open ball condition $v(x-1)\ge N$. The power image is a union of its cosets, hence open. For finiteness, the map $\mathcal O^\times\to F^\times/P$ kills $V_N$ by the unit root just constructed. Each $x\in F^\times$ has unique coordinates $x=\pi^a u$ with $a\in\mathbb Z$ and $u\in\mathcal O^\times$. Divide $a=r+nb$ with $0\le r<n$, also when $a<0$. The factor $(\pi^b)^n$ disappears, proving the displayed surjectivity. In the code, $\pi^b$ is the explicit power witness in the quotient equality. This argument uses finiteness of the residue field for the index, discreteness for the uniformizer and integer division, completeness for the root, and the nonzero **field** cast for $d$. [Lean, read] `LocalFields/UnitsPowers.lean:195–235`, `LocalFields/UnitsBasic.lean:145–257` and `LocalFields/ExistsUnitFiltrationUnitsLeAndRangePowLe.lean:12–47,49–59,108–125,194–200`. $\square$

**Lemma 2 (finite Kummer cohomology).** For the original $U$ and root module, $Y$ is finite and killed by $n$.

*Proof.* The $n$-th power sequence on $\Omega^\times$ is short exact since every $T^n-a$ with $a\ne0$ is separable and has a root in $\Omega$. On invariants the unit group is $F^\times$. Exactness and $H^1_{\mathrm{cts}}(U,\Omega^\times)=0$ give
$$
F^\times\xrightarrow{(\cdot)^n}F^\times
 \xrightarrow{\kappa_n}Y\longrightarrow0.
$$
For $a\in F^\times$, $\kappa_n(a)$ is the class of $g\mapsto g(A)/A$ for $A^n=a$. Another root changes it by a coboundary, and choosing the product of two lifts proves $\kappa_n(ab)=\kappa_n(a)+\kappa_n(b)$. Thus it factors through $F^\times/P$, where its kernel is trivial by exactness. The proof of finiteness in Lean uses the surjection alone. It packages the map as a homomorphism from units into the multiplicative wrapper of $Y$, using the map from fixed-field units, $(H^0\text{ comparison})^{-1}$ and the connecting map. The image of the power homomorphism lies in its kernel. It descends by the quotient lift and gives a preimage of $y$ by choosing a unit representative for $\kappa_n$. Lemma 1 supplies the finite domain. The quotient is killed by $n$, as is $Y$. [Lean, read] `LocalTateDuality/EqZeroOfForallCupPairingZmodMulEqZeroLeft.lean:23–66` and `LocalTateDuality/KummerCup.lean:109–150,181–189`. $\square$

**Lemma 3 (finite character count).** For $n\ge1$ and a finite abelian group $A$, write $A^\vee_n=\operatorname{Hom}(A,\mathbb Z/n)$. Then $|A^\vee_n|\le |A|$. If $nA=0$, then $|A^\vee_n|=|A|$.

*Proof.* Postcomposition with $j_n$ is injective: $[m/n]=[0]$ exactly when $n\mid m$. Next send $f:A\to\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z$ to the character $a\mapsto\exp(2\pi i f(a))$ into $\mathbb C^\times$. The exponential here is well defined on a rational class and has trivial kernel. Distinct characters $\chi,\psi$ are orthogonal in $\mathbb C^A$. In fact $\lambda=\psi\chi^{-1}$ is a nontrivial character. For a $b$ with $\lambda(b)\ne1$, translation gives $\sum_a\lambda(a)=\lambda(b)\sum_a\lambda(a)$, so the sum is zero. Values have absolute value one by finite order, and each character has squared norm $|A|$ with the unnormalized inner product. Taking the inner product with one character at a time gives linear independence of every finite family, so there can be at most $|A|$ characters. This is the code's inequality, with an intermediate rational-to-real circle embedding and Mathlib's normalized inner product.

For equality on a finite group killed by $n$, one may use the finite abelian group decomposition $A\simeq\bigoplus_i\mathbb Z/d_i$. Each $d_i\mid n$. An image of the generator of $\mathbb Z/d_i$ is an arbitrary element of $(\mathbb Z/n)[d_i]$, precisely a multiple of $n/d_i$. There are $d_i$ choices, independently for all $i$. The empty product gives one for the trivial group. More generally the count by cyclic factors is $\prod_i\gcd(n,d_i)$. Equality under the torsion hypothesis is useful for reading the examples. The archive's perfect-pairing proof uses the upper bound valid for every finite $A$. [Lean, read] `PoincareDuality/Torsion.lean:14–39,90–100`, `TorsionDualFinite.lean:14–59,115–124` and pinned Mathlib, `Analysis/Fourier/FiniteAbelian/Orthogonality.lean:35–86`. $\square$

**Theorem (two finite adjoints, both onto).** For every $K,\Omega,U,n$ as above, $X$ and $Y$ are finite $n$-torsion groups, and $B(x,y)=t(\beta(x,y))$ induces isomorphisms
$$
L:X\longrightarrow Y^\vee_n,\quad x\longmapsto (y\mapsto B(x,y)),
\qquad
R:Y\longrightarrow X^\vee_n,\quad y\longmapsto (x\mapsto B(x,y)).
$$
Equivalently, each character on $Y$ is $B(x,-)$ for exactly one $x$, and each character on $X$ is $B(-,y)$ for exactly one $y$.

*Proof.* The cup and $t$ are additive in the indicated arguments. If $L(x)=0$, evaluation at every $y$ and injectivity of $t$ give $\beta(x,y)=0$ for every $y$. The left annihilator input gives $x=0$, and applying this to differences proves injectivity of $L$. If $R(y)=0$, evaluation at every $x$ gives the right annihilator premise by the same injectivity. It gives $y=0$, so $R$ too is injective. Since $Y$ is finite by Lemma 2, so is $Y^\vee_n$ by Lemma 3, and $L$ makes $X$ finite. The trivial coefficient group already makes $nX=0$.

Now both sides of the following chain are ordinary finite cardinalities:
$$
|X|\le |Y^\vee_n|\le |Y|\le |X^\vee_n|\le |X|.
$$
All four inequalities are equalities. The image of $L$ has $|X|=|Y^\vee_n|$ elements, so it is all of $Y^\vee_n$. The image of $R$ has $|Y|=|X^\vee_n|$ elements, so it is all of $X^\vee_n$. Thus there are two surjections, and the injections give their respective unique preimages. This is `IsPerfectPairing` in the archive. In lines 129–139, `t` is the invariant as an additive homomorphism and `β.flip` exchanges argument order while preserving the given scalar value. In lines 140–149, the first finiteness result is applied to $Y$, the injection gives finiteness of $X$, and the two character bounds give the reverse cardinal inequalities required by the two bijectivity calls. The proof works for $n=1$ with all groups trivial. It includes mixed characteristic with $v(n)>0$ and makes no assumption that $F$ contains a primitive $n$-th root. [Lean, read] `LocalTateDuality/EqZeroOfForallCupPairingZmodMulEqZeroLeft.lean:117–149` and `PoincareDuality/NsmulEqZeroOfNonemptyAddEquiv.lean:115–130,154–182,201–222`. $\square$

**Computing the matrix in the archive's normalization.** We use the positive Kummer cup comparison and the arithmetic Artin evaluation as named inputs. For $x$ a continuous $\mathbb Z/n$-valued character on $U$ and $a\in F^\times$ the comparison takes $x\cup\kappa_n(a)$ to the cyclic class $a\cup\delta(j_n\circ x)$ in the units module after scalar restriction to $G_F$. Its local invariant is $(j_n\circ x)(\operatorname{Art}_F(a))$, with $\operatorname{Art}$ sending a uniformizer to arithmetic Frobenius on unramified layers. The equality defining $t$ and injectivity of $j_n$ therefore give
$$
B(x,\kappa_n(a))=x(\operatorname{Art}_F(a)).
$$
On the right we evaluate in the finite abelian quotient through which $x$ factors. Each computation below takes $K=F$ and $U=G_K$, the top open subgroup, and all finite extensions are inside the same $\Omega$. [Lean] `LocalTateDuality/KummerCup.lean:80–101,193–252`, `LocalClassFormation/CupSymbol.lean:60–163,173–177`. [Milne, *Class Field Theory*, III §3, Proposition 3.6 and Lemma 3.7, pp. 109–110](https://www.jmilne.org/math/CourseNotes/CFT.pdf)

**Example 1 (prime modulus, $F=\mathbb Q_3$, $n=2$).** Every principal unit is a square by Lemma 1 with $d=0$. The unit quotient is therefore $\mathbb F_3^\times/(\mathbb F_3^\times)^2$, generated by $-1$, and valuation gives the independent generator $3$. The classes $y_1=\kappa_2(3)$ and $y_2=\kappa_2(-1)$ generate $Y\simeq(\mathbb Z/2)^2$ by Kummer exactness.

Let $x_1$ be the character of $F(\sqrt{-1})/F$ with value $1$ on the nonidentity element. The polynomial $T^2+1$ has no root in $\mathbb F_3$ and is separable there, so the extension is unramified quadratic. Arithmetic Frobenius is nontrivial and units have zero image there. For $x_2$ use the character of $F(\sqrt{-3})/F$ with value $1$ on the sign change. The polynomial $T^2+3$ is Eisenstein, so the quadratic extension is totally ramified. The norm of $\sqrt{-3}$ is $3$. To rule out $-1$ as a norm, write $a+b\sqrt{-3}$ with $a,b\in F$. Its norm is $a^2+3b^2$. The two terms have valuations of opposite parity whenever both are nonzero, so the valuation of the sum is the smaller one. If the norm has valuation zero, then $a$ is a unit and $b$ is integral. Its residue is $1$, excluding $-1$. The norm criterion, $t$ injective, and the two-element target give $B(x_2,y_1)=0$ and $B(x_2,y_2)=1$. Thus the matrix in the ordered lists $(x_1,x_2)$ and $(y_1,y_2)$ is
$$
M_2=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}\pmod 2 .
$$
The two characters have order two and the matrix proves their independence. Since $|X|\le |Y^\vee_2|=4$, they give all of $X$. A functional on $Y$ with values $(a,b)$ on the generators is represented by $ax_1+bx_2$, and a functional on $X$ with those values is represented by $ay_1+by_2$. [Paper] [Serre, *Local Fields*, I §6, Proposition 17 and III §5, Theorems 2–3](https://www.sas.rochester.edu/mth/sites/doug-ravenel/otherpapers/Serre-Local.pdf)

**Example 2 (composite modulus with wild ramification, $F=\mathbb Q_5$, $n=10$).** The valuation coordinate is $\mathbb Z/10$. For the units, let $i\in\mathbb Z_5$ be the root of $i^2=-1$ with $i\equiv2\bmod5$, given by the simple residue lift. Its powers map bijectively to $\mathbb F_5^\times$. Every unit factors uniquely as $i^e u$ with $e\bmod4$ and $u\in V_1$. The power $10$ on the order-four factor has the same image as the square, so $[i]$ has order $2$. On $V_1$ the square map is onto $V_1$ by the simple root lift at $1\bmod5$. For $a\in\mathbb Z_5$,
$$
(1+5a)^5=1+25a+125h(a),\qquad h(a)\in\mathbb Z_5.
$$
The fifth powers lie in $V_2$ and their images fill $V_2/V_3$ by the displayed congruence. Moreover, the rescaled root argument of Lemma 1 applied with exponent $5$ and $d=1$ gives fifth roots of $V_3$ in $1+25\mathbb Z_5\subset V_1$. Given $w\in V_2$, choose $a$ to match its coefficient modulo $V_3$ and correct the remaining factor by such a fifth root. Hence $V_1^5=V_2$ and also $V_1^{10}=V_2$. Since $6=1+5$ maps to $1$ under the coefficient map $V_1/V_2\to(\mathbb F_5,+)$, the ordered Kummer generators
$$
(y_\pi,y_t,y_w)=\bigl(\kappa_{10}(5),\kappa_{10}(i),\kappa_{10}(6)\bigr)
$$
have orders $10,2,5$ and generate the direct product of those cyclic groups. In particular $|Y|=100$. The field $F$ itself has no primitive fifth root, as $\Phi_5(1+T)$ is Eisenstein at $5$.

For the rows take the following continuous characters.

- $x_\pi$ factors through the unramified extension of degree $10$ and sends arithmetic Frobenius to $1\in\mathbb Z/10$. Its values on the Artin images of $5,i,6$ are $(1,0,0)$.
- $x_t$ factors through $F(\sqrt5)/F$ and assigns $5\in\mathbb Z/10$ to the sign change. The root has norm $-5$ and $-1=i^2$ is a norm (of the scalar $i$), so $5$ is a norm. For the norm $a^2-5b^2$ to have valuation zero, the same parity test as above forces $a$ to be a unit and $b$ integral. Its residue is a square, which excludes $i$ of residue $2$. The scalar simple residue lift gives a square root of $6$ in $F$, so $6$ is a norm. The row is $(0,5,0)$.
- Let $E=F(\zeta_{25})$. The polynomial $\Phi_{25}(1+T)$ is Eisenstein. Its reduction is $T^{20}$, by reducing $\sum_{j=0}^4(1+T)^{5j}$ modulo $5$, and its constant term is $5$. Thus $E/F$ is totally ramified of degree $20$. The automorphism $\zeta_{25}\mapsto\zeta_{25}^b$ identifies its Galois group with $(\mathbb Z/25)^\times$. This group is the direct product of the order-four subgroup lifting $\mathbb F_5^\times$ and the order-five kernel of reduction. The latter is generated by $6\bmod25$, since $6^j\equiv1+5j\bmod25$. Use the character $x_w$ of this Galois group that assigns $2\in\mathbb Z/10$ to $6$ and kills the order-four subgroup. Its fixed kernel field is a totally ramified extension of degree $5$. This is an actual wild character because the residue characteristic is $5$. For arithmetic Artin the action of $5^a u$ on $\zeta_{25}$ has exponent $u^{-1}\bmod25$. The values on the three chosen classes are $(0,0,-2)=(0,0,8)$: $5$ acts trivially, $i\bmod25$ has order four, and $6$ acts by $6^{-1}$. [Paper] [Milne, *Class Field Theory*, I §3, Examples 3.8 and 3.13, pp. 40 and 44](https://www.jmilne.org/math/CourseNotes/CFT.pdf)

In the given row and column orders the pairing matrix is
$$
M_{10}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\0&5&0\\0&0&8\end{pmatrix}\pmod{10}.
$$
Here the diagonal entries generate the allowed image subgroups of orders $10,2,5$ in $\mathbb Z/10$. If a linear combination of the three characters is zero, evaluating on the three columns forces its coefficients to vanish modulo $10,2,5$ respectively. They generate a subgroup of order $100$, and $|X|\le |Y^\vee_{10}|=100$ gives all of $X$. To exhibit both actual surjections in these coordinates, the possible characters on either group have generator values $(a,5b,2c)$ with $a\bmod10$, $b\bmod2$ and $c\bmod5$. On $Y$ these values are represented by $a x_\pi+b x_t+(4c)x_w$, since $8\cdot4c=2c\bmod10$. On $X$ they are represented by $a y_\pi+b y_t+(4c)y_w$. Both formulas give unique representatives in the listed cyclic coordinates.

**What the hypotheses control.** Fix $n\ge2$. Remove finiteness and let $X=Y=\bigoplus_{j\ge1}\mathbb Z/n$ with the pairing $B(x,y)=\sum_j x_jy_j\bmod n$. The sum is finite and testing against the vector supported at coordinate $j$ with value $1$ detects $x_j$ or $y_j$. Thus both annihilators vanish, and both sides are still killed by $n$. Every functional is specified by its values on those single-coordinate vectors, so the dual is $\prod_{j\ge1}\mathbb Z/n$. Each adjoint has image exactly the finite-support sequences. The functional sending every vector of the indicated basis to $1$ is missed on both sides. This uses ordinary additive duals of discrete groups, exactly as in the finite pairing. In the finite argument one finite side suffices, since an injection into its finite dual already supplies finiteness of the other side.

For the same target $\mathbb Z/n$, deleting an explicit $n$-torsion hypothesis while retaining bilinearity and the two zero annihilators cannot produce a counterexample among finite abelian groups. For any $x,y$ one has $B(nx,y)=n B(x,y)=0=B(x,ny)$. Hence the zero annihilators imply $nx=0$ and $ny=0$ for every $x,y$. Indeed, the counting proof applies to arbitrary finite abelian groups even before this consequence is observed. This closes the proposed deletion route with that target. The role of $n$-torsion before currying is different: the formula $(j,\zeta)\mapsto\zeta^j$ on a coefficient group requires $n$ to kill that group for independence of the integer representative of $j$. For example the representatives $0$ and $2$ of $0\bmod2$ act differently on a generator of $\mathbb Z/4$.

The field-cast hypothesis has its own obstruction. If $F=\mathbb F_p((T))$ and $n=p$, taking $p$-th powers yields exactly $\mathbb F_p((T^p))^\times$. For distinct positive integers $a<b$ prime to $p$, the quotient $(1+T^a)/(1+T^b)$ has first nonconstant term $T^a$, so the classes $[1+T^a]$ give infinitely many distinct cosets. Also $T^{1/p}$ is purely inseparable of degree $p$, so the $p$-power map on $F^{\mathrm{sep},\times}$ fails surjectivity. The Galois root group is trivial in this case. This pinpoints the failed power and Kummer steps of the stated theorem.

**Remark on precedence and use.** The finiteness and cup-duality conclusion is the classical specialization of Tate's local theorem at the trivial finite module $\mathbb Z/n$ in complementary degrees $(1,1)$. Its Cartier dual is $\mu_n(\Omega)$ by evaluation at $1$. The conclusion over $G_F$ transports to $U$. A rational-valued character on a group killed by $n$ has values in $(\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z)[n]$, which is identified with $\mathbb Z/n$ by $j_n$. Tate's formulation and Milne's proof specify invertibility in the field, so the wild mixed-characteristic example belongs to the specialization. [Paper] [Tate, *Duality theorems in Galois cohomology over number fields*, §1 and Theorem 2.1, pp. 288–289](https://math.stanford.edu/~conrad/BSDseminar/refs/TateICM.pdf) · [Milne, *Arithmetic Duality Theorems*, 2nd ed., I §2, Theorem 2.1, Corollary 2.3 and Remark 2.5(b), pp. 26–29](https://www.jmilne.org/math/Books/ADTnot.pdf)

**Actual consumer.** `LocalTateDuality/MainDefs.lean:88–95` installs the theorem at the field `isPerfectPairing` of `localDualityHypotheses`, for every open subgroup and this same invariant. `PoincareDuality/Devissage.lean:99–112` uses the corresponding hypothesis at the chosen open subgroup $W$ when checking degree-one duality for coinduced trivial cyclic coefficients. The broader bundle uses its own cohomological finiteness, degree-three vanishing and invariant corestriction inputs. The readings here are author-level local R3 for the named production bodies and immediate field assignment. [Lean] retains the named class-formation, general coefficient local duality and global Poitou–Tate suppliers. [Open] marks the proof-reading boundary at their deeper bodies.
